- **The kind `ingest`.** `runtime/step_ingest.py` wraps the operations of `runtime/ops_sources.py` (`import_version` and `recheck`), so ingestion is the engine's second consumer. Arguments `{"source", "file", "sha256"}`, `{"source", "url"}` or `{"source"}` (a refresh). A file is never read from an arbitrary path of the machine: `file` is a plain name, read from the task's own file drop (`.workbench-local/drop/<task id>/`, filled with `hand-over`, which now accepts a step task), and only when its sha256 is the one the plan carries; another hash stops the task (`file_changed`). The footprint is empty, since the task writes nothing in the project, and nothing a step leaves is written into the sources folder but the version `import_version` makes. A refusal of the import is recorded as the closed code of the operation's exit (`usage`, `folder` or `refused`), with the sentence that says to run `source-import` in the terminal for the reason.
- **The plan.** A plan task for a step is `{"key", "step", "args", "footprint", "agent": "runtime", "title", "text", "depends_on", "milestone"}`; the plan shows the steps (kind and arguments) beside the table and the hash covers them; "at least N runs" counts skill tasks only. `plan.from_flow` accepts a step in a loaded flow, and `plan.combine` passes it. The flow file schema (`step` and `args` in `flows/*.json`, parameters) is P6's; this package does not change `runtime/flow_files.py`.
- **Not done here, on purpose.** The file-output kinds of P8 and the project script of P9 register in the same way and are not built; nothing but tests creates a step task until P6 gives the flow files the key `step`. No page shows a step's kind (the `status` task row has no new field), and a step task leaves no run row, so the `ran` list of a dispatcher round does not report its end (the round's `started` list, `status` and `task` do); a skill task does not wait for a step task that writes a file it reads (the derived waits read skills' declared outputs; only the admission rule weighs a step's footprint).
- **What changed no band:** no skill was edited or bumped, no fingerprinted file changed.

## 2026-10-15: follow-up of the step task (P5c)

Small package after P5 (the reviewer's minors). Nothing here changes a decision; the rule for the review stop stays as P5 left it (P8 makes "a review must be harmless to pass" a rule in code).

- **The dispatcher's flag, asked again under the lock.** The dispatcher starts `run-step --unattended`; that process asks the brake (`steps:mode`) and the kind's `unattended` again under the claim lock, before it claims, and holds the task (`stopped` or `start it by hand`, left `ready`) when either changed since the round decided. A person's `run-step` and `run-next` carry no flag and ask neither. `run-task` on a step task is the dispatcher-style call (it carries `checked`), so it asks too.
- **The brake on the page.** The `agents` read carries `steps` (`{"agent": "runtime", "mode": "autonomous"|"stopped"}`), with or without area agents. The Building's panel has a "Step tasks" section: the state in a sentence and one button, "Stop step tasks" (a narrowing, always allowed) or "Resume step tasks" (the policy's `set-mode:up`; without it a line names the terminal command). One request, `POST /agents/runtime/mode`.
- **`ingest` reads its file safely, once.** The drop folder is opened by descriptor, the name looked at without following a link and refused before it is opened unless it is a regular file (a pipe would wait for a writer for ever), opened `O_NOFOLLOW | O_NONBLOCK`, compared with the first look (same device and inode), read at most `drop.MAX_BYTES` (a larger file is `file_too_large`), and hashed. The bytes that were hashed are the bytes imported (`ops_sources.import_bytes`, the non-operation twin of `source_import`), so a file swapped after the check changes nothing. Rolling back an import after the fact was not possible: versions are immutable (limit L13 family).
- **The write helper's errnos** are the platform's own (`errno.ELOOP`, `errno.ENOTDIR`), not numbers.
- **What changed no band:** no skill, no fingerprinted file.

## 2026-10-15: workflows with steps, parameters and dates; the flow check; save a plan as a workflow (P6a)

Package P6a of the delegation platform plan (decisions D1 to D39 stand; this one carries out D27, D31, D32, D33 and the flow half of D23). `contracts/runtime.md`, "Workflows", is the map.

- **Where a project's workflows live.** `docs/workbench/flows/<name>.json` in the project. Chosen over `.workbench-local/` because the person versions a workflow (it is a decision about how work is done, not work data), and `.workbench-local/` is git-ignored. A run cannot write there (path-rule class `other`, as for `runtime.json`). A name of a workbench flow is never read from the project.
- **The schema.** A task is a skill task or a step task (`skill` xor `step`); step kinds come from the engine's registry. A skill task may list `sources` (ids; the workflow pins the id, never a version) and say `"web": false`. The sources a task receives are those it lists plus the `ingest` steps before it. Parameters are declared and filled by `{name}`; `{today}` and `{week}` are filled from the clock of the machine, in its own time zone, which the plan states. The plan hash covers the bound tasks. Rejected: a templating language, a `{{` escape (a brace group around a lowercase word that is not declared is a problem), and typed parameters beyond `text` and `file`.
- **The flow check and the split.** A skill that requires the web (`requires` holds `search:web`) and receives sources is refused when the workflow is saved or checked. The task is "marked web" by its skill, not by a key of its own: a `web` key on a task can only be `false` (run without the web). When the skill lists a research brief among its inputs (today `biz-market-analysis` and `biz-icp-positioning`), the code offers the split of D31; for any other skill it asks the person. In manual use D16 stands (the plan card highlights, the person decides).
- **A migration for the word `web: false`.** The plan said P6 takes migration 13 for routines. The split's second task has to run without the web its skill requires, and a plan card that says "Web: no" while the run still has the web would be a lie, so the run must read the word from the store: `tasks.web_off` (NULL or 1). It is migration 13; the routines of P6b take migration 14, and addendum 12 (the `drive` source type) goes there. A run without the web of a skill measured with it is never `proven`: it is supervised, and the consent of D38 can still release it.
- **A file for an ingest step (addendum 13).** The file is handed over for the request before the plan (`stage-file`, `.workbench-local/drop/request-<id>/`), a `file` parameter names it, and the plan carries its hash; approving checks it again and moves it into the drop of the task that reads it. Chosen over asking for the task's drop first (the task does not exist) and over letting the step read any path (a hard limit of P5). A plan made another way keeps P5's hand-over after the approval.
- **A workflow is planned.** `request --flow` still starts a workbench flow of skills with no parameter, no source and no `web: false`; a project flow, a flow with a step, with parameters, with a task that lists sources or says `web: false` goes through `route --flow` and the plan's hash (D37). Such a flow started at once would lose the word `web_off` and the sources, and a failing flow would start with neither the flow check's finding nor the D16 highlight in front of the person. A project file with a workbench flow's name is refused by `request` as by `route`.
- **What "receives" means for the check.** A task receives the sources it lists and those of the steps before it that bring one in; a step kind says so itself (`Kind.brings_source`, true for `ingest`), and `sources_of` and the check read that, not the name. A source that reaches a task through an artifact (a research task that depends on a task which received a source and reads what it wrote) is outside the check, as D16 speaks of what a task receives, not of what it may read. The sources a bound flow lists must be ids the project holds or ones a step of the flow brings in, checked when the plan is built (as the router's choice is).
- **The case of a path.** The path rule compares `docs/workbench/runtime.json` and `docs/workbench/flows/` in lowercase: on a case-insensitive disk a run's `docs/workbench/Flows/x.json` would be the project's workflow. Such a path is class `other`, so it is not returned and, through the same class, never enters a change set. The check is on the path rule, the one place both ways back read.
- **Files handed over for a request are cleaned.** `.workbench-local/drop/request-<id>/` is removed when the plan is rejected, when the request is cancelled from the terminal or the page, and after an approval (the file the plan names is copied to its task first, the others are dropped). The approval never fails after it commits: a file that cannot be read then is reported in `files.failed`. A request cancelled by the task board's sync leaves its folder (it is git-ignored and in the project's own `.workbench-local/`).
- **Saving a plan keeps a file parameter.** A plan built from a workflow with a file parameter is saved with that parameter (`{survey}`, `{survey.sha256}`), not the file's name and hash. Text parameters of a plan are saved as the values the plan carried.
- **Save this plan as a workflow (D32).** `flow-save --id <approved plan>`; a task without a text of its own gets `{request}`, a parameter whose default is the request's words. The page shows the control under the resolved line of an approved plan; scheduling it on the same screen comes with the routines (P6b).
- **Not done here, on purpose.** Routines, the hash of D35, the catch-up rule and the messaging limit (P6b). The router does not choose among project workflows (an X change of `core-orchestrator`): the person names one on the page or in the terminal. The check of a workflow as a routine's target (no file parameter, no web with sources at each fire) is P6b's.
- **What changed no band:** no skill was edited or bumped, no fingerprinted file changed.

## 2026-10-15: routines: a workflow on a schedule, approved by the hash of its content (P6b)

Package P6b of the delegation platform plan (decisions D1 to D39 stand; this one carries out D23, D24, D31 to D35 and the routine half of D16, and closes findings S3, S10, I5, F4, F12). `contracts/runtime.md`, "Routines", is the map.

- **Migration 14, not 13.** P6a took 13 for `tasks.web_off`. Migration 14 holds the routines, their fires and the `drive` source type (addendum 12): the `sources` table is rebuilt inside the migration's transaction with `PRAGMA defer_foreign_keys = ON` (the foreign keys of `source_versions` are checked at commit, when the rows are back), the Drive rows are rewritten to type `drive` and the bare id, and the two triggers are made again; a test shows the versions and the records of what runs received are unchanged. `sources.public_row` is the identity now.
- **Where the code lives.** The spec proposed `runtime/handlers/routines.py`. A handler is a subprocess that imports nothing of `runtime/`, and a fire needs the plan builder, the proof and the store, so the logic is `runtime/routines.py` (pure) and `runtime/ops_routines.py`, and the tick is called by the dispatcher's round itself (`ops_runs._fire_routines`), not by a configured handler. A failure of a routine never stops a round.
- **The record.** A routine is a draft until approved; approval stores the hash and the content it covers; any change clears both. Cancelled, not deleted (a trigger refuses the delete). A fire is a row keyed by (routine, slot) written with its request in one transaction.
- **What a stop is.** The routine becomes `stopped`, a fire row `asked` is written, and a request with a `question` is created. Chosen over a new pending-decision kind (a migration of the CHECK, and every shell would need to learn it) and over a plan card (the tasks may not be buildable): a question on a request that waits for its route is an existing shape the store already accepts, the messaging channel already notifies, and the answer only closes. Approving the routine again cancels that request. The cost is that the request shows among the requests that wait for a route until then; its title says what it is.
- **The catch-up rule (the plan's proposed default, confirmed while the maintainer is away, 2026-10-15).** After an outage the newest missed slot is run once if it is younger than a window the record holds (default: the routine's period), the older ones are skipped and noted, and if the newest is older than the window all are skipped and noted. Never once per missed slot. The dates of a late fire are those of the fire, per D33 ("filled at fire time"), not of the slot.
- **Schedules.** Three forms: every day at a local time, every week on a weekday at a local time, every N minutes (15 to 720, dividing a day). Slots are named by their local date and time; the zone is the machine's, taken from the clock of the fire. Daylight saving is not modelled: a slot is a wall-clock time.
- **The consent of a routine (D34), distinct from the agent's (D38).** Same text (the constant of P3, whole), but its own field in the routine's record, entering the hash with the warning's hash; given with `--consent` in the terminal or by the page with the warning's hash, never from messaging, only for a fixed model; a change of the model clears it. The agent's consent is not read for a routine's task, and every request a fire creates carries the routine's model word, so an agent's fixed model cannot decide for it. A review of a routine's task is released by the routine's consent only while the routine is active and carries it.
- **`auto` and the providers.** The approved provider set is the gate file's routes (`direct` names a model with none), required for an `auto` routine. At a fire each task's model is the cheapest reliable pair inside the set; if the unrestricted choice is a pair outside it the routine stops and asks.
- **Limit L33 (S10)** and its mechanism: the operation checks the channel it is told it came from, against the workflow it makes and the one it changes, computed from the checkout (`Kind.networked`) and the store (an active standing approval of the agent that owns a skill with a side effect). `Kind.networked` is new and false for every kind today; the project script of P9 sets it. Rejected: a rule that reads the channel policy only (a policy can grant what this limit refuses).
- **A routine refuses a file.** A workflow with a `file` parameter cannot be a routine's target: nobody is there to hand the file over (addendum 14).
- **Not done here, on purpose.** The project script's hash and declared destinations are a slot in the content (`scripts: []`), for P9. The router does not choose among routines or project workflows. The question of a stop is not a pending-decision kind of its own. A request cancelled by the task board's sync leaves a routine's stop request as it is.
- **What changed no band:** no skill was edited or bumped, no fingerprinted file changed.
- **Review of P6b.** (1) A routine's slots count from the later of its approval and its last resume (`routines.resumed_at`, set by the resume): what passed while it was paused, or a draft after a change, is not made up. (2) For `auto` every skill task is pinned to a model inside the approved providers; a skill with none stops the routine and asks. (3) Consent for a routine that follows the project's default model writes the default's id on the record (so the consent names its model, and `consent_in_force` can hold); the hash covers the resolved id, never an alias or the word `default`; the page's "New routine" and "Schedule it" lead to a working path. (4) An image that cannot be looked up defers the slot (no row, retried at the next tick, a line in the round) and an approval refuses to record it: the hash of a routine never holds a digest of nothing. (5) The fire is written only while the routine is active and approved with the hash the tick read. (6) One routine's error is its own line and the others still fire; a stop is written under the claim lock. (7) A step of a kind this checkout lacks is refused by L33's reason and by the routine. (8) Changing or cancelling a routine closes the request of its stop, as approving does.

## 2026-10-15: the project script as a step: approval by hash, a sandbox, a per-step proxy, hashed dependencies (P9)

- **A changed script fails and is planned again; it does not ask a question.** The hash of the script, of its destinations and of its lock file are arguments of the plan, so the plan hash covers them. When the file no longer hashes to the approved value the step fails with `script_changed` (or `lock_changed`) before anything is staged, and its note tells the person to read the file and plan the step again with `prepare`; the new hash is then approved by a plan decision, which only the terminal and the page approve. Chosen over a `question` decision answered with `continue`, because the default policy of the messaging channel answers questions and a chat message must not be able to approve new code. The coordinator accepted this deviation from "stops and asks (a pending decision)" of the spec.
- **Not unattended; a source maker.** The kind never starts on the dispatcher's own; the kind is `networked` (always, since it cannot know at registration whether a task declares destinations), so a routine whose flow holds one is made only from the terminal or the page (L33), and the routine's approved content holds each such step's script sha256, destinations, lock sha256 and the script image's definition hash (`Kind.routine_slot`); a routine that fires finds the task held `start it by hand`, and running a script without a person is a later decision. It declares `brings_source`, so a flow's flow check sees the source its result becomes a version of.
- **A script's network is structure, not a prompt.** `--network none` by default; with declared destinations an `--internal` network (read back as `Internal` before anything starts on it) and a per-step proxy of the same image that resolves the declared names once, refuses an address that is not public, and serves CONNECT to a declared pair or a frozen address only. Destinations are TLS endpoints and public hosts: the stricter rule on purpose (principle 13); a LAN service cannot be declared.
- **Dependencies are an image layer.** A build container with no mount reads the lock on standard input and installs wheels only, with hashes, through a per-step proxy to the package index; its file system is kept with `docker commit` (its configuration cleaned) and cached by the lock's sha256. Chosen over a host folder, which would need a host extraction of untrusted files.
- **The helper is not loosened.** `providers/_container.py` still has no switch for the network; the runner replaces `--network none` in its own `shape()`, only with the step's internal network.
- **Limits L34 to L38** (numbers moved from L33 to L37 after P8 took L30 to L32 and P6b took L33). **What changed no band:** no skill was edited, no fingerprinted file changed, no migration.
- **Review round: the gateway of the internal network.** A plain `--internal` bridge keeps the host's own address on it (the gateway answers ARP and resets any port), which reached every service the host listens on without the proxy. The step's network (and the build's) is created with `com.docker.network.bridge.gateway_mode_ipv4=isolated`. What the code checks, not what it hopes: the daemon is 28.0 or later (`docker version`; older ones ignore an unknown bridge option and still echo it, and forward DNS: CVE-2024-29018), and the network read back is internal, `.EnableIPv6` false, with no `Gateway` in any entry of `.IPAM.Config`, and the option in `.Options`. A docker test probes the subnet and the neighbours table, and another reads back a plain and an isolated network from the real daemon.
- **The image is part of the approval.** The plan's arguments carry `image`, the definition hash of the script image; `verify` and `Kind.vet` compare it with the checkout's files (`image_changed`), so a rebuilt image does not run new proxy code under an old approval. The base image's label is compared with the whole hash, as the dependency image's is.
- **A changed file fails the task; it does not open a decision** (kept, accepted by the supervisor): a decision answerable from messaging would let a chat message approve new code, and the plan that approves it is approved in the terminal or on the page.
- **Review round: plan-time checks and image labels.** `Kind.vet` (steps.py) lets a kind read the files its arguments name when a plan is built and before it is approved (`ops_flows.vet`, `before_approve`); the project script uses it for the hash, the credential scan and the lock form. A dependency image is used only with the approved lock's sha256, and both its labels (the lock, the definition) are compared. The build's `/tmp` is 512 MiB. Known and written: the proxy's boundary is an address and a port, not the TLS server name; the staged path is visible in `/proc/self/mountinfo`.
- **A plan that holds a networked step is approved only at the terminal or on the page.** A script with declared destinations can POST to them, an external effect outside the effect gate, and plan `approve` is open to messaging by the channel policy. So `ops_waits.only_on_plans` refuses, by default, for a plan whose tasks hold a step of a `networked` kind (or of a kind this checkout does not have), any channel but `operations.NETWORKED_PLAN_CHANNELS` (terminal and page; one named literal, which a later policy makes a grant, as the maintainer decided on 2026-10-16 for every channel restriction), with the reason and whatever the messaging policy grants today; no channel at all is refused too (it fails closed). Part of limit L36, no new number. The same set of channels already holds for a routine that holds such a step (L33).
- **A stop reaches the script runner's objects.** `run_stop.snapshot` also reads `openhora-script-{run,proxy,build}-<16 hex>` from the `docker run` lines below the holder and reports `script:<token>` in the stop's runs; `orphans.remove_runs` removes the containers and networks built from that token, by exact name. The eval sweep's own rule (a name of `wb-eval-` only) is unchanged. This is the backlog item R19 of #443, done by this package and removed from the backlog.

## 2026-10-15: gitleaks as an optional second secret scanner, and the days to answer a report (S18, T28 part a)

Package S18 of the delegation platform plan, https://github.com/chrissgon/openhora/pull/420. `ops-repo-baseline` goes from 1.0.0 to 2.0.0 (class X: Stop rule 3 changed).

- **Next to the bundled scanner, never instead of it.** `secret_scan.py` stays on in step 2, in the hook and in the workflow: it needs nothing from outside and knows the project's planted fixtures (`kind`). gitleaks is the person's choice, asked in step 3 with `bundled` as the recommendation; the gate is a stop of Stop rule 3, not an open question in a draft, because step 3 already stops before any file is written.
- **Pinned, verified, adopted (principle 14).** gitleaks 8.30.1 (published 2026-03-21), read on 2026-10-15 from the project's releases page and its checksums file; the four platform checksums are in `skills/ops-repo-baseline/references/gitleaks.md`, `scripts/gitleaks_setup.py` (the installer: the person runs it, the skill and the hook never do), the workflow asset (linux x64) and the hook asset (the version); a test fails when they disagree. The workflow downloads the archive and checks its sha256 before running it. The project's GitHub Action is not used: it needs a license key on organisation accounts and is a moving dependency.
- **The configuration** extends gitleaks' default rules and allow-lists only the folders of `PLANTED_DIRS` of the bundled scanner (a test keeps the two equal). The consequence is stated in the reference: a real secret under `tests/`, `fixtures/`, `evals/` or `examples/` is asked about by the bundled scanner and not seen by gitleaks, which is why the bundled scanner stays on.
- **T28 (a): the days to answer a report are asked, never defaulted.** A decision of step 3; Stop rule 3 adds that a "go" never answers a commitment only the user can make (this and the owner's handle). The recommendation is a rule (the shortest period the user can keep for every report), not a number.
- **What the bundled scanner allows, gitleaks does not know.** gitleaks reads neither `.secret-scan-allow` nor `kind`, so an allowed documented fake or a kept revoked key would keep its step and the hook red. The skill proposes a `.gitleaksignore` line (`<commit>:<file>:<rule>:<line>`, the fingerprint from the user's first local run with `-v`) for each such finding, added only after the user agrees, says the gitleaks step stays red until then, and never widens `gitleaks.toml`.
- **Cases.** Case 1's prompt now states the scanner (otherwise the skill rightly asks); cases 4 (gitleaks chosen), 5 (no period given) and 6 (a documented fake allowed in `.secret-scan-allow`) are new. Nothing was run: the pending runs are the battery's full test, or `--cases 1,4,5,6 --baseline` on the lab models.
- **Not verified.** The gitleaks binary was not downloaded or run; its flags come from the README and source of the v8.30.1 tag. **What changed no band:** no fingerprinted file and no evidence changed; the skill needs a full test (X change).

## 2026-10-15: outputs to the documents platform: a new page, a new table, rows added (NOTION-OUT)

Package NOTION-OUT, an extension of P8's write-back (decision D17, direction 2), on the maintainer's decision of 2026-10-15 that the platform creates pages and tables from a task's results, so that a weekly report can end as a table the team already uses. `docs/architecture/platform/outputs.md`, "A new page, a new table, rows added", is the map.

- **One word, four operations.** The side-effect vocabulary is unchanged: the word stays `write`. The effect document gained `operation` (`update_page`, P8's, now carrying the key; `create_page`, `create_table`, `append_rows`) and the hash covers it. The three new operations have their own document (`runtime/output_document.py`), dispatched by `effect_write`; the P8 document and its tests are unchanged but for the key.
- **Two new step kinds, not an extension of `write-back`.** `output-page` and `output-table` (one module, `runtime/step_output.py`). A write-back replaces text and its arguments, checks and preview are about that; each new shape has arguments of its own. Neutral names: the product is behind the provider. The gate step is called `approval` (the literal `write` is forbidden in a step module by the static test of L30, which stays as it was).
- **Rows come from a CSV file or the first sheet of a workbook, read by code.** The workbook is read with the standard library (`zipfile`, `ElementTree`), not with `openpyxl`: the reader runs in the host process, so it takes no dependency, refuses an entity declaration, a DOCTYPE, a bomb and an error cell, and reads only what a table needs. Rejected: the converter's container (a second image and a round trip for a table of 500 rows) and a library in the host.
- **The column rule is code and says what it did.** Title: the named column, else the first. Checkbox, number, date, url only when every filled cell fits (a checkbox only with no gap); everything else text; select only when named. An ambiguous column is text and the preview says "ambiguous". Rejected: inferring a select from repeated values, and a number from `1,200` or `007` (a zip code is not a number).
- **Idempotency lives in the provider's ledger, not in a column of the person's table.** The key is derived from the document, so one document is written once however many decisions reach it. A row of unknown outcome is looked for in the table and adopted if it is there; a page or database of unknown outcome is adopted only if exactly one child of the parent matches, else the key waits for `resolve`. Rejected: a hidden property holding a row key (a column nobody approved, in a table other people use). Residual risk, stated in the docs: a listing that lags can make one row of unknown outcome be written twice.
- **Caps.** 500 rows, 30 columns, 2,000 characters a cell, 1 MiB a table; rows added only to a table of at most 2,000 rows. Chosen from the platform's rate (one call a row, 2.8 calls a second: about three minutes for 500 rows, inside a 900 s limit for the long verbs) and its limit of one text object; the provider repeats the checks and a test holds both sets of numbers equal. A longer file is split into files of 500 rows, each approved on its own.
- **A stale table.** For rows added, the table's columns (hashed), its row count and the time of its newest edit are read with the task and again before the write; a different one answers the decision and sends the task back to `prepare`. For rows already begun by this document only the columns are compared, so that a failed run is finished without being mistaken for a person's edit.
- **L31 extended, no new limit.** The row now says that it holds for the four operations. `L30` stays: `step_output.py` has a row in the static test's table of imports; `DocumentsReader` gained the read `table-read` (its three verbs are all reads) and nothing that writes.
- **Not done, on purpose.** No call to a real workspace: the endpoints were read in the public reference on 2026-10-15 (`providers/documents/README.md`) and exercised against the stand-in service only, which was extended for databases and data sources. No Drive output. A table of several data sources, relations, formulas and rich cell formatting are not made.
- **Review round 1 (four major findings and the minors).**
  - *Rows an earlier approval wrote (M1).* A task that started over after the table changed got a new key and wrote its rows again. The provider's ledger now records each row with the hash of its values and the task's scope (`task-<id>`); a row of the new attempt that hashes like a row of the same scope under another key is that row, and is not sent again. `prepare` counts them (`rows-state`, a read of the ledger) into `base.rows_done`, the preview says "N of these M rows are ALREADY in the table", and the stale answer says that an earlier attempt wrote rows and never "nothing was written". The key now includes the cycle. Rejected: a key that leaves the base out (a changed file would still duplicate) and reading the effect files of the earlier cycles from the step (a step reads no effect file but its own).
  - *Dates (M2).* A lost-answer row was matched on the raw text of a date, but the service writes a date-time back with milliseconds and `+00:00` for `Z`. Dates are compared as dates and date-times as moments, and the stand-in service normalises like the service. A date-time must carry its Z or offset (the alternative, sending the runtime's zone, needs an IANA name that the runtime does not keep): one without is text, with a note, and a column named as a date stops the step.
  - *A bomb in the column index (M3).* A cell reference such as `ZZZZZ2` set the width of a list. A cell with no value is ignored whatever its reference says, a letter run of more than three is refused, and a cell with a value beyond column 30 is `too_many_columns`, before any list is built.
  - *The write lock (M4).* The remote write of an output (minutes) no longer holds the project's write lock. It runs under the lock of the decision (`output-<id>.lock`), the effect lock of the request and the provider's ledger; the write lock is taken for the reads, the approval row and the record. A write-back (one call) keeps its lock. A decision rejected during a write was already possible before (reject takes no write lock); the record then fails, and the ledger holds what was written.
  - *Minors.* Cross-key claims: a row found in the table is never one that any entry of the ledger holds. The resume check is stated exactly in `outputs.md` (the row-count window and its gap: one person's row added while a row of unknown outcome is pending is not told from it). New select options the platform adds to a person's table, formulas with no cached value, the sheet read and the hidden sheets skipped are remarks in the document and the preview. The names the platform gave the parent or the table are in the document and next to the ids. An entity or DOCTYPE is refused in any encoding (a NUL byte is refused, so UTF-16 cannot hide one); every zip fault (`BadZipFile`, `RuntimeError`, `NotImplementedError`, `zlib.error`, `EOFError`) is `xlsx_unreadable`; a negative or past-the-end string index is refused; the first visible sheet is read. A cell is counted in UTF-16 code units, the larger count, since the reference says "characters" and not in which unit. Done ledger entries of the outputs are dropped after 90 days. A page or a database the person put in the trash is made again instead of replayed (the cycle in the key serves a task that started over; the trash check serves the rest).
- **Review round 2 (three major findings, three minor, and the nits).**
  - *Reject during a long write.* `reject` and `answer` of an `effect` decision a step task opened now try the decision's lock without waiting (`output-<id>.lock`, which the approval of an output takes first) and refuse "being written; wait for the write to finish" while it is held. Before, a reject returned `cancelled` while the call went on and made the table, and the record then failed. The lock file is removed with the lock (as the last act while it is held, the order `ops_core._try_lock` depends on). A write-back keeps the write lock for its one call and has no such lock.
  - *The ledger is not the table.* A row counted as done because the ledger said so, even if a person had trashed its page: the preview said "1 of 2 rows are ALREADY in the table", the stale answer "they stay there", and approving wrote only the other. Now an entry counts as done only if the page it made is among the rows the table lists (`rows-state` for the preview and the check, the write itself, and the search for a row of unknown outcome all list the table once: at most 2,000 rows, a longer table is refused, not guessed at). `rows-state` therefore reads a credential; the dry run of `rows-append` stays offline and says `verified: false`. A trashed row is a row to write again. A task whose counted rows are fewer than at its preview is stale and sends nothing. The query's `created_time` filter is no longer used (the listing is compared client-side), one API shape less that the reference confirmed only in part.
  - *Two projects, one ledger.* The ledger is one file for the person and task numbers start again in every project, so project B's task 1 found project A's rows. The document has a `store` (the first 12 hex characters of the sha256 of the store path, as the key of a write-back has) and the scope is `s<store>-task-<id>`; the key includes it.
  - *Minors.* A key with an entry of unknown outcome is never pruned (its done rows would be written again), and an output decision older than 90 days is refused. A provider that outlives a killed runtime takes an exclusive lock of its key for the life of a write, so a second one is refused. `instant()` returns no moment at the edges of the calendar instead of raising. A cell with a value and no reference is refused. The wording about the write lock now says what the code does: only the record at the end takes it.
- **Review round 3 (cancel during a write, and two minors).**
  - *Cancelling the request during a write.* Cancel is a brake, so it is not refused: `runtime/ops_cancel.py` `request_cancel`, the one function the four ways of cancelling a request call (`ops.cancel`, the board's `cancelled` move, the direct reply, a routine closing its stops), takes the lock of each open effect decision without waiting and passes the ones it cannot take, the writes that run, to `store.request_cancel(keep=...)`. That cancels everything else and leaves the decision and its task alone; the running write records its outcome when it ends and the request, cancelled meanwhile, stays cancelled. The answer says "the write of <target> is running and will be recorded; everything else is cancelled". The locks taken are kept until the cancel is done, so no approval begins between the look and the cancel. Changed for the later merge with CANCEL-BOARD: `store.request_cancel` (a `keep` argument; `kept` in the answer only when something was kept), the four call sites (one line each), and the new `ops_cancel.py`. Rejected: refusing the cancel while a write runs (a brake that may be refused is not one).
  - *The record of a write that landed* waits for the project's files without a limit (`wait=None`); the usual 60 seconds could leave the decision open after the table was made.
  - *The provider's lock* is of the scope of the task when the call gives one, so a new key of the same task is held back by an orphan still writing under the old one; and the pruning groups a key's entries by the key (`<parent>/<key>` and `<source>/<key>/row-<n>` share it), so a pending database keeps its rows.
- **What changed no band:** no skill was edited or bumped, no fingerprinted file changed.

## 2026-10-15: two validator checks, agent-skill and capability-invokes

Package PLVAL of the delegation platform plan closes the last two rules of the backlog item PL-validation in `scripts/validate.py`.

- **`agent-skill` (an error).** Every skill an agent cites exists. Two forms are read. A name in `metadata.skills` of `agents/<name>.md` (a list on one line or a block list) must be a folder `skills/<name>/` with a `SKILL.md`: the runtime stages each entry as a skill folder, so a flow file is no entry. A backticked name with an area prefix in the body, outside code fences, must be such a folder or the bare name of a flow file `flows/<name>.json`; `flow-<name>` is no alias of a flow file (it would let `flow-brand` pass). A body citation followed by `(planned)` (as in the routing table), or on a line with `validate: allow agent-skill -- <reason>`, is not read. **Not read:** the `description` of the frontmatter, a name not in backticks, a path, `/name`. The finding names the agent, the line and the name.
- **`capability-invokes` (a warning).** Principle 4 had no check. The rule reads only the `SKILL.md` of a skill of kind `capability` other than the router, and only these sentences, where `<name>` is another built skill: "run|invoke|call|use|execute [the] skill `<name>`", "... [the] `<name>` skill", "invoke `<name>`", "ask the skill `<name>` to", "`/<name>`" and "hand it|the request|the task over to `<name>`". Not read: the frontmatter, code fences, table rows, `## Gotchas`, a line with `validate: allow capability-invokes -- <reason>`, and a sentence that addresses the user: "tell|ask|recommend|suggest (that|to) the user|person" with no comma before the invocation, "the reply says|names|tells|asks", a "Next:" or "Recommended:" line. A bare "user", "person", "next" or "say" exempts nothing: "When the user approves, invoke the skill `<name>`" is a finding. Citing a skill as the owner of an artifact or as the one to run first ("stop and tell the user that `<name>` writes it") is the convention and is not a finding.
- **Why a warning, and why narrow.** A broader pattern ("run `<name>`") also matches the reply templates that tell the user which skill to run and "run after `<name>`", which is timing, not an invocation. Both the narrow rule and a manual pass with the broad reading found no invocation in the 48 skills (the broad one matched only reply templates, timing and flow skills, which may invoke), so the rule is a guard for new text; a true miss is a pattern added later. What it saves is noise on a convention the skills already follow; what it gives up is the invocations written in words the patterns do not list, which the lab's cases and a review still catch.
- **Not verified.** The rule reads prose by pattern: a skill that invokes another in words the patterns do not list is not found. No skill was changed and nothing was run in the lab.

## 2026-10-15: a password manager as the fourth place a secret is looked up, 1Password first (S16)

Package S16 of the delegation platform plan, as preparation for the launch with companies. The page `docs/architecture/platform/unattended.md` ("Use with 1Password") is the map; the contract is `contracts/secrets.md`, "The password manager".

- **The order is fixed: environment, credentials directory, OS secret store, then the password manager.** It adds a place and replaces none, with no migration. The person cannot change the order: one order everywhere is the contract, the environment first lets CI and a cloud session override, and a configurable order doubles the test matrix. The cost, a stale OS secret store entry that shadows the manager, is shown and not hidden: `--list` and the doctor say where each secret was found and mark a name that is also mapped ("mapped in the password manager, not asked: found earlier"), with no call to the manager.
- **The mapping is explicit and outside the repository.** `$XDG_CONFIG_HOME/openhora/password-manager.json`, else `~/.config/openhora/password-manager.json` (both variables are already in `ENV_MINIMUM`, so no new variable): `backend`, an optional `account`, `timeout_seconds` (1 to 60, default 15) and `references` from a secret's name to a reference. It holds references and never values. Only a mapped name is ever asked of the manager, so without the file the behaviour is exactly the earlier one. A convention of vault and item names was rejected: a company's vaults have their own structure, and it would ask the manager (and perhaps a biometric prompt) for every registered name.
- **The file is checked like the credentials directory** (not a link, a regular file, this user's or root's, mode 0600, 64 KiB, `O_NOFOLLOW`), and a file that fails, is not JSON, has an unknown key or backend, or holds a reference that fails the syntax check is state `unsafe` with the reason and the fix, and the manager is not called. References are not secrets, but a writable mapping can point a name at another item (a read token swapped for a write one). The check of the folder that holds the file is not made (only the file's own owner and mode), as for the credentials directory's files.
- **The call.** `op read <reference>` (and `--account <a>` when set) as a list of arguments, no shell, stdin on `/dev/null`, a deadline, an environment of `PATH`, `HOME`, `USER`, `LANG`, `TMPDIR`, `XDG_CONFIG_HOME` and `XDG_RUNTIME_DIR` only when set, plus `OP_SERVICE_ACCOUNT_TOKEN` only when the resolver itself found it (environment, credentials directory or OS store; never through the manager, and it cannot be mapped, so there is no recursion). A reference is `op://<vault>/<item>[/<section>]/<field>` with the vendor's documented set of characters (letters, digits, spaces, hyphens, underscores, periods); nothing before `op://`, so nothing can read as an option. Standard output over 64 KiB is refused; standard error is read for fixed markers and dropped.
- **Sign-in is the person's.** Supported: the desktop app integration (a laptop) and a service account token (a headless company machine). Not supported now: `op signin` sessions (`OP_SESSION_*`, which expire in minutes and which `provider_environment` strips) and a Connect server. Nothing in the workbench signs in, adds an account, unlocks the app or touches the terminal.
- **The states fit the existing words.** `found` (source `password manager (1password)`, no reference printed); `missing` (the tool answered that the item or field does not exist); `locked` (not signed in, locked, the prompt dismissed, the deadline, and also the tool not installed or an error the markers do not recognise: never `missing`, the lesson of I3); `unsafe` (the mapping file). A failing backend leaves its own sentence (`_FAILURE` keeps the kind and the sentence), so the three messengers, the operations layer and every caller change nothing. The store locked and the manager finding the secret is `found`; the store empty and the manager locked is `locked` with the manager's sentence; both failing, the store's sentence comes first. In a report, once the manager has failed it is not asked again for the other mapped names, as for the store.
- **No cache.** Each lookup calls the tool once: a long-lived service would keep every secret in its memory for its lifetime, and a rotated secret would be served stale. The cost is one process per lookup (to verify on a machine: roughly a second, and a service account's request limits). The runtime keeps its 30 second cache of the probe only (`op whoami`, 5 seconds), never of a value. A cache, if ever needed, is in process memory, at most 60 seconds, never on disk, and a later decision.
- **The structure is provider-agnostic.** `providers/secrets/password_managers.py` (standard library, Python 3.9 syntax, loaded by path by the resolver) holds `BACKENDS = {"1password": OnePassword}`; a backend has `reference_ok`, `read`, `probe`, `classify` and `token_name`. Bitwarden (`bw get`) is a second class and a word of the mapping's `backend`; the resolver's chain and its callers do not change. Adopt the vendor's tool, build the link (principle 14).
- **Limit L39 (principle 11).** A password manager's tool is called only by the resolver, on the host, without a shell, never to sign in or unlock, and never inside a run container: the lookup answers `unavailable` without calling anything when `WB_EVAL_CONTAINER` is set, the backend refuses to start the tool in that case too, and no `OP_*` variable is in the executor's `FORWARD`, a gate model's `pass_env` or an adapter's eval block (read; `evals/executor.py` is unchanged). L34 to L38 belong to P9. Test: `test_limit_39_password_manager_never_in_a_run`.
- **A defect found on the way.** `providers/resolve.py` `ENV_MINIMUM` did not keep `OPENHORA_CREDENTIALS_DIR` or `CREDENTIALS_DIRECTORY` (the second is what a systemd unit sets), so a provider started with `provider_environment` could not find a credentials directory that only those variables name, and its secret read as missing; the credentials directory is where the service account token lives. Both carry a path and never a value, and they now pass. A provider could already read the default `~/.config/openhora/credentials` through `HOME`.
- **The doctor.** A check `password_manager` beside `secret_store` (`na` without a mapping, `ok` when `op whoami` answers, `warn` with the fix for locked, unavailable and unsafe), and a `locked` line that prints the failing backend's own sentence instead of a fixed one about the keyring.
- **What changed no band:** no skill, no evidence, no migration and no fingerprinted file changed. The exact texts and exit codes of `op` are read from the vendor's documentation and one public report where they exist; the rest are marked to verify on a real machine.

## 2026-10-15: the maintainer's decisions on the doubtful backlog items

Taken in chat during the cleanup of the documents (package CLEAN, `docs/backlog.md` edited in the same pull request). Each decision is in the item it concerns; this entry says what was decided and why, and where the text of a closed item is.

- **Closed.** T15 (the concurrent `init` of the store): fixed on the hypothesis in pull request #25, no recurrence in two weeks, and the test prints the stderr of each process, so a new failure opens a new item with its cause. T30 (rename "flow" to "workflow"): the word is "flow" everywhere (code, files and text for users); the recent "workflow" wording is replaced by package FLOW-WORD (backlog FLOW-WORD), after the cleanup and FLOWS1. R10 (the provider classes an agent's first task needs): principles 5, 8 and 14 already cover the rule, so it is not recorded elsewhere. S16, S18 and PL-validation were closed by their own pull requests (#421, #420, #423). The text of all six is in `git show 156a08cb:docs/backlog.md`.
- **Kept, with a trigger.** T7 (routing per pack): when a real case of a wrong skill being loaded appears, or when a pack grows past an agreed size; never in the battery. T14 (open items as issues): near future, at the official launch of the platform. S17 (apply the host settings): a real company that needs to standardise many repositories; the manual checklist stays the default. R9 (scenario evals): the first real company process proved, the weekly-report flow (Drive spreadsheets, approval on WhatsApp, saved back to Drive), built with a fictional project's data. N4 (the grader's agreement with itself): measured once after the battery, on about 10% of its replies, split by the run's model, only on the maintainer's word.
- **Kept and rewritten.** T28 in two parts: (a) before the battery, `ops-repo-baseline` asks for the disclosure period instead of writing "7 days" (done with S18, #420); (b) after the battery, the second model's results for five skills are read again and only the ones that lapse again are repaired. PB15 (the weekly vote) is in use by a project of the maintainer, with a post scheduled for 2026-10-23 09:30: the first runtime cannot be deleted (CONS-2b) before that project moves to the task runtime's handlers.
- **To build.** R12 (the skills a run loaded, on follow-up runs) and N14 (a local model, its server in its own container with no route to the host) before the battery, with the measurement changes of T23, in one execution-side epoch. R13: the runtime keeps its nine states in its own select and also writes the board's native status by status group (to-do, in progress, complete), a setting of the project, off by default. S16 and S18 were built now for the launch with companies. Agent memory (the rest of R2) is replaced by per-project standing instructions (`docs/workbench/instructions.md`, written only by the person, loaded in every run, never overriding a stop rule or an approval, protected from runs, with a size cap), package INSTRUCTIONS (backlog INSTRUCTIONS).
- **New skills and flows.** Group A (NS1, NS2, NS12, NS13, NS14, `mkt-landing-page`, `mkt-analytics`, `design-user-research` and the missing phases of the business plan) is created after the walk of the backlog and a last review of the architecture, with the maintainer's real cases; a quick test first, the full test only when the quick one is acceptable. Group B (NS3 to NS11) are flow files, not skills; their tests are code tests. Step 1 is NS4, NS5, NS6, NS10 and `flows/fix-bug.json`; NS7 is a decision of the planner; NS11 is written flattened.
- **Rejected.** Deleting the first runtime before the weekly vote moves (it would stop a running vote); a model test for the flow files (a flow file is read by code); several full tests of a new skill (one quick test finds the problems cheaper).
- **Not verified.** Nothing in this entry was run; no skill, evidence or fingerprinted file changed.

## 2026-10-16: the skill creator on the per-model runner, a linked worktree in project init, the dated fixtures +11 years, "work data" (SKILLFIX: T32, N5, T29, T22)

Package SKILLFIX of the delegation platform plan, https://github.com/chrissgon/openhora/pull/424. `core-skill-creator` goes from 3.1.0 to 4.0.0 (class X), `ops-pull-request` from 1.0.0 to 2.0.0 (class X), `core-project-init` from 1.0.0 to 1.1.0 (class Y). No lab run was made: the full tests and the partial test wait for the battery, which runs every skill anyway (principle 12).

- **The skill creator describes the runner as it is (T32).** No reference model and no floor model: a skill is done when its first full test has passed the gate on every model of `lab_models`; each pair of skill and model has its own band, gate and score, and no model decides another's; a test of another model is the person's own and writes local evidence, which never makes a skill done. Stop rule 3 shows `eval_run.py --skill <name> --dry-run --harness <adapter> --model <model id>` (one `--model` per model, the first is the lead model) and asks for the adapter and the ids, or the gate file; it never chooses them. The test table of the output template has one row per variant and model, and the Gate line one result per model. `--tiers`, `--floor-harness`, `--floor-model`, `.floor` slots, `early_ends.<tier>` and every mention of `result.json` are gone; every option the three files name was looked up in the `--help` of its tool.
- **The size trim moves depth, not contract (N5).** `SKILL.md` goes from 20,474 to 19,443 characters by the validator's count, under the size guide with a margin. The detail of steps 7, 8, 10 and 11 is in the references (a new "The Security Step" in the authoring guide; "Eval Cases", "Refine with Real Execution", "Analyzing Patterns" and "Iteration Loop" already held the rest); each step keeps its action, its command and its stop-rule reference. The stop rules, the reply that asks, the output template's fields, the quality criteria and the External-content line keep every rule.
- **`.git` as a file is version control (T29).** A linked worktree or a submodule keeps a regular file named `.git` whose first line is `gitdir: <path>`. `init_project.py` counts it when the path (relative to the root or absolute) names an existing folder; a file of another content, or one naming a missing folder, does not count, so a stray file cannot make a folder a project root. The check reads the file and the folder and calls no `git`.
- **The dated fixtures move +11 years, to the horizon 2038-10-02 (T22).** The cases whose skill or script compares a date of the material with the day of the run (a slot in the past, an expiry still ahead, a plan that starts after today) carry every year of their material +11, names included. 2026 and 2037 both start on a Thursday and 2027 and 2038 on a Friday, none is a leap year, so every weekday and every interval is kept and only year digits change; +6 years (2033) was rejected because it breaks weekdays. 301 ISO dates were checked by a script, 0 mismatches. A recorded hash that depends on the shifted files (the approved plan of `mkt-publish`) is recomputed. The cases kept out are those whose dates are document stamps nothing compares with today, or whose prompt states today. `mkt-vote-round` 3 left the year of "mon oct 12" to the real clock, so its prompt now names it. No 32-bit epoch handling exists in the changed skills, their stand-in providers or `providers/` (Python and the 64-bit container); the launchd scheduler refuses a time more than 330 days ahead, which no case reaches.
- **"Work data", and why `ops-pull-request` is X.** The platform's data under `docs/` is called work data in `core-project-init` and `ops-pull-request` (the platform's flows are not "workflow data"; GitHub's CI workflows keep their name). The line of `ops-pull-request` is Stop rule 5, inside `## Stop rules`, so the change is X, not Y, and the validator would refuse a declared Y there; the line is not left unchanged to save the test, because the battery runs a full test of every skill anyway.
- **Done on every model needs a baseline on every model.** A full test runs the baseline on the lead model only; the gate of another model is `not computed: no baseline in force on <model>` until `--baseline-on <model>` has run, so step 9 of the skill adds that option for each other lab model, and the Gate line of the output template has the value.
- **What changed no band:** no fingerprinted file, no evidence and no `versions.jsonl` line by hand; the changed cases lose their evidence and baseline and are `pending` until the battery runs them.

## 2026-10-16: a flow is a file; the flow-fix-bug skill is removed; the packs of the engineering agent widen (FLOWS1: NS4, NS5, NS6, fix-bug)

Package FLOWS1 of the delegation platform plan, https://github.com/chrissgon/openhora/pull/426. `core-orchestrator` goes from 3.0.0 to 4.0.0 (class X: its Stop rule 2 now says that a flow file the routing table lists without `(planned)` counts as installed). No lab run was made: `core-orchestrator` 3.x has no passing full test, so the battery's full test pays for 4.0.0 at no extra cost.

- **A flow is a file, not a skill.** The standard flows are `flows/<name>.json`, planned by code at once with no model call and routed as `flow-<name>`. `flows/build-feature.json`, `improve-code.json`, `new-project.json` and `fix-bug.json` join the existing ones; the routing table lists the built flow files. The validator reads a `flow-<name>` route as built when a flow file exists, warns on a `(planned)` row whose flow file exists, and refuses a flow file and a skill of the same route.
- **`flow-fix-bug` the skill is removed (version 1.2.0).** Its nine phases are `flows/fix-bug.json` (five tasks: the four optional phases are not tasks, a static file cannot read the plan's conditions). Its cases and its evidence are in git history, at the parent of the pull request (`git show aa31cfca:skills/flow-fix-bug/SKILL.md`). 47 skills remain. Its 30 lines of the endings corpus went with it, and the floor of the recognised stops fell from 0.77 to 0.76 (measured 0.765).
- **The packs widen, and why.** A flow file may only name skills of a pack in use with a whole runtime manifest (`runtime/tests/test_runtime_manifest.py`). So `packs/code.txt` holds nine more engineering skills and `packs/design.txt` holds `product-backlog`, each with a whole manifest under `evals/` (backlog R16 progress). An autonomous agent's consent is per agent, model and mode (`runtime/autonomy.py`), so the consent an engineering agent already holds now also covers these skills' tasks; effects keep needing the approval of their exact content by hash.
- **`new-project` ends at the backlog.** The runtime adds one `eng-implement` sub-task for each todo task of a released backlog; a static implementation task would have implemented the first task twice. The dependency rule treats a required row of alternatives as met by one upstream path and names a writer that only comes later (`reorder the tasks`).
- **Not built:** NS7 (routing a ticket is the planner's decision) and NS10 (`mkt-publish` is a code path in no pack, and no built skill makes a post asset; it waits for a `publish` step kind). `site/` keeps its dated snapshot of the proof data, which still lists `flow-fix-bug`.

## 2026-10-16: the process diagnosis is the platform's starting point (NS40)

- **What.** A person describes their processes; the AI writes a plan from the catalog of skills and flows: what covers each step, its proof per model, the gaps, where to start. It executes nothing. Maintainer's decision; area-neutral; first tried with small accounting and law offices.
- **A capability skill, `core-process-diagnosis`**, not a mode of `core-orchestrator` (its own trigger, artifact and stop rules: design rule 4; the router stays a one-request hand-over), not an agent (no lab test reaches an agent: principle 9), not a flow skill (it runs no phase).
- **The catalog is computed, not remembered.** `scripts/skill_catalog.py` writes it from the frontmatters, the flow files, the router's planned rows and the bands the status script or the proof file computed; it never computes a band itself. The diagnosis names only what the catalog lists, and cites the catalog's commit and date (principle 10).
- **Gated on real cases.** The skill is written after the processes of one person who works in law (every process they know) and a hand pilot corrected by them; the script is built now because it is deterministic and is the pilot's tool.
- **Name, gaps, bands:** `core-process-diagnosis`; a step no planned skill covers is "not covered, nothing planned" with a `Skill gap:` line, never an invented name; the diagnosis document shows every model of the gate file, `reliable` first (the catalog itself keeps the gate file's order). The diagnosis is written in the project's language, which a runtime configuration key will define (a later package).
- **What changed no band:** no skill, no evidence and no fingerprinted file changed.

## 2026-10-16: one base resolver, and a bump that refuses a base that is not the change's base (BUMP-GUARD)

Package BUMP-GUARD of the delegation platform plan, https://github.com/chrissgon/openhora/pull/432. A reviewer reproduced a destructive `bump`: on a branch cut from the integration branch, with no `WB_BASE_REF`, the base fell back to the merge base with `main`, `bump` raised the version from `main`'s line, and wrote "the base's lines plus the new line" over the version file, dropping the integration branch's lines and rewriting `version:` to a lower number. The validator and the pre-commit hook used the same base, so correct work on such a branch failed the hook ("N lines were added"), which pushes an author toward the destructive bump or toward skipping the hook. CI was safe: it sets `WB_BASE_REF` to the pull request's base.

- **One resolver, `resolve_base` in `evals/eval_status.py`.** `bump`, the validator's class checks, the strict reading of new evidence files and the hook read the base from it. The first step that applies decides: `WB_BASE_REF`; the key `workbench.baseRef` set for one worktree (`git config --worktree workbench.baseRef origin/feature/delegation-platform`, or `branch.<name>.workbenchBaseRef`; never the clone-wide config, which every worktree and the maintainer's live checkout share, so a clone-wide key is read only while the clone has one worktree; both are ignored on the default branch); the branch's upstream, unless it is the branch itself or its own pushed copy under another name (recognised only when its tip is an entry of the branch's own work in the branch's reflog: a commit, a cherry-pick, a revert, an am, a rebase that made a commit, a merge that made a merge commit; a creation point, a fast-forward and a rebase onto the upstream's own tip before the branch had a commit of its own are not, so an integration branch the branch was cut from, pulled, rebased onto or merged forward stays the base, while a pushed tip that came from merging the integration branch forward is the branch's own; and `push.default=upstream`, which makes `@{push}` equal `@{upstream}`, changes nothing); the worktree key is read on a detached HEAD too; the merge base with the default branch, as before. A variable or a key that names nothing is an error, never a fallback to the next step (a wrong base must not silently become another). `bump` prints the base it used on stderr; the validator prints it as a `NOTE`.
- **`bump` refuses (exit 2, nothing written) when the version file is not the base's lines plus at most one.** It is checked before `SKILL.md` or the version file is touched. The message names the base compared with, `WB_BASE_REF` and `git config --worktree workbench.baseRef`. It also refuses when the new version would be lower than the one `SKILL.md` or the line the file already has beyond the base holds, for every base, named or implicit. Only when the base came from the implicit steps (the upstream or the default branch) does it refuse as well when that one extra line was added by a commit another ref contains (a base exactly one line behind, a detached HEAD on the integration branch, a stale upstream); a base named by `WB_BASE_REF` or a key is not second-guessed on that check, because a branch whose own line is pushed to another name or copied would otherwise be locked out of a second bump. An idempotent re-run (the base's lines plus the one line this change added) is unchanged, and so is a first-line bump of a new skill.
- **Why a refusal and not a guess.** The saving of guessing the base (no setting to make) is smaller than the loss of safety of a wrong one that rewrites an append-only file (principle 13); the upstream step makes the common case (a branch cut with `git checkout -b <name> origin/<integration branch>`) right with no setting at all.
- **Not built:** the resolver does not look for the pull request's base on the host (no network call); a branch cut from a local branch with no upstream and no key still falls back to the default branch. `bump` refuses it when the file differs from the base by two lines or more, when the extra line is on another ref, or when the version would fall; a base one line behind whose integration branch exists in no ref of the clone and whose version does not fall (the same class as the integration line gives the same version) is not caught, and is left to the validator's class check and to CI, which sets the base.
- **What changed no band:** no skill, no evidence and no fingerprinted file changed (`eval_status.py` is not in the fingerprint). Tests: `evals/tests/test_base_resolver.py`.

## 2026-10-16: flaky tests with root causes, the Python 3.12 float test, the drop folder by descriptors, images of earlier definitions (T35 to T38)

Package TESTFIX of the delegation platform plan. No skill was edited, no band moved, no fingerprinted file changed (`python3 evals/eval_status.py` reports the same fingerprint).

- **T35: each flaky test got a reproduction, a root cause and a fix that removes the cause.** (a) The lock that changed hands: three windows, all reproduced on purpose (a delay between the second holder's lock and its write: 10 of 10 failed with signal 9; a probe holding the lock when the second holder tried: 10 of 10 failed). The third came from the first repair attempt: a fork made by one thread while the main thread holds a probe's descriptor copies it, a flock belongs to the open file, so the child holds the lock until it has exec'd and `stop_children` reads "held" with the file still naming the first holder (1 to 2 of 100 under load). Repair in the test: the second holder is started before the stop and waits on a line, writes its owner line before it takes the lock, retries the lock until a deadline, and is let in on the first holder's own "released" line, never on a probe. The code did not change: `stop_children` never signals a new holder; the process it can KILL after the TERM is the one that got the TERM, when the lock reads held while the file still names it (its docstring says so). (b) The Discord test: the previous test left its worker thread running, and the worker posted through the next test's router; the listener keeps its threads (`Listener.threads`) and the rig waits for them. (c) The parallel-runs test: a barrier of marker files replaces a one-second sleep and a wall-clock bound; its other flake was a real race in `evals/execution.py` `load_executor` (the module was registered before it ran, so a second thread saw a module with no `read_meter`), now under a lock like the other loaders in that file (infrastructure, not in the fingerprint). (d) The markdown and the stripper timing tests compare growth, not a clock: the work at half the size against the work at the size (processor time, the best of several, a collection before each render), with one generous absolute bound against a hang; the shapes that allocate most are measured at a size where the working set fits in cache, because at the larger size the time grew far more than twice under a busy machine for a reason that is the machine's, not the code's. (e) The site tests click the ribbon's badge to take over, and wait until the demo's own `elementFromPoint` at the aimed point answers with the intended element before every click. Counts before and after are in the pull request.
- **T36: fixed in the test, because the code does not depend on the version.** Python 3.12 made the built-in `sum()` of floats compensated (Neumaier summation: "What's New in Python 3.12", the entry for `sum()`, gh-100425), so `sum([0.8] * 6)` is `4.800000000000001` there and `4.8` before, and the premise `six < 0.8` of the test no longer held. `at_threshold` and `within_tolerance` compare with `FLOAT_SLACK = 1e-9`, which does not depend on the last bit. The test now builds the value one float step below the threshold with `math.nextafter` and checks the mean of six runs whatever the version gives. **A finding left open, not fixed here** (the fix is a change of the band rule, not fingerprinted; it moves to the pre-battery measurement package, whose spec takes it): the `thin baseline` cause of the band compares `gate["with"] - gate["baseline"] < margin` (`evals/eval_status.py`, margin 0.2 in `evals/eval-gate.json`) with no slack, on means that are sums of floats; in 20000 random pairs of case means (values such as 0.6, 0.7, 0.75, 0.9, over 3 or 6 runs) 26 gave a different answer on 3.11 and on 3.12 (for example a mean of 0.9 against a baseline whose runs sum to 0.6999999999999998 on one and 0.7000000000000001 on the other), and the means printed to three decimals do not differ. A mean exactly one margin above the baseline can therefore carry the `thin baseline` cause on one version and not on the other. The gate itself (`gate_passes`) and the 0.70 line (a pessimistic score rounded to four decimals) are not affected. Also found, and also moved to that package: `scripts/tests/test_stage_skills.py::test_a_link_inside_a_skill_is_copied_as_its_content_and_never_points_back` fails on Python 3.9 (`shutil.copytree` on a dangling link in `scripts/stage_skills.py`, a fingerprinted file); it was already failing on the base, so this package did not cause it (backlog T40).
- **T37: the drop folder is opened through descriptors.** One opener (`drop.open_folder`) goes from the project's root to `.workbench-local`, `drop` and `<task id>` one part at a time with `O_NOFOLLOW`; a file is read with `read_file` and written with `write_new` from that descriptor. Every user goes through them (the hand-over, the listing, the run copy, the ingest step, the staging of a request and the placement at the approval); the run copy now receives the very bytes that were scanned, from a copy outside the project. No caller opens a drop path by its string.
- **T38: the images of earlier definitions are removed on the person's word, never automatically.** `python3 scripts/doctor.py --remove-old-images (--dry-run | --confirmed)`, selection rule and reasons in `docs/architecture/platform/unattended.md`. It is never automatic because an old image can be wanted again (rebuilding costs minutes and the network), a person decides what that is worth, and the removal cannot be undone by waiting; the doctor's `images` row only tells. A provider that builds an image adds its name to `IMAGE_FAMILIES` in `scripts/doctor.py` with its own test; only the converter and the exporter are there.

## 2026-10-16: per-project standing instructions, written only by the person (INSTRUCTIONS, limit L40)

Package INSTRUCTIONS of the delegation platform plan (SPEC-INSTRUCTIONS). The maintainer's decision replaces "agent memory": a file of the project, like a chat product's custom instructions, that every model run of the project reads. Rejected: a memory table the assist fills; a file the runs may edit; one file for the project's technical rules and its instructions.

1. **Path and owner.** `docs/workbench/instructions.md`, plain Markdown with no header, written by the person (by hand, on the page, in the terminal). A row `user` in the layout contract's "provided by" table; no skill declares it. Why: the person must read and edit it where the runs read it (no read-only mirror beside a hidden source).
2. **Who reads it.** Every model run of the project: task runs, the router's run and contained runs. Not: step tasks (code), the first runtime's `run-agent.sh` (being retired), and the lab, whose cases are fictional projects, so a run with instructions is not the measured condition; a test holds that `evals/` and `runtime/lab.py` never name the module or the path. Known limit: field evidence does not record whether instructions were present (a backlog item under INSTRUCTIONS, not built here).
3. **The block.** Empty when the file is absent or blank, so a prompt is then byte for byte what it was; otherwise the person's text verbatim between two separator lines, under a sentence that says it never replaces a stop rule, a confirmation gate, an approval or a limit of the runtime, that a skipped instruction is named in the reply, and that the person's later answer wins. Its head and tail carry a code made for each block (`secrets.token_hex(8)`, in no file), and the sentence says only the block with that code is the person's, so a forged copy of the markers in other text of the prompt is not. It sits right after the "For this task:" paragraph; after the router's task text; after a contained run's prompt. The run folder's `prompt.md` already keeps the exact text, so the store needs no column.
4. **Checks, read and write alike.** UTF-8, a regular file, not a link, inside the project, 16 KiB read at most; the credential scan; no invisible character; no line that opens as a separator of the prompt (`--- text`, in any dash, after any list, quote or emphasis marker, folded as a reader sees it) and none that holds the words of the block's own marker; at most 4,000 characters. 4,000 is the cap the conversation's memory for the router already has, so the router's added share stays bounded and known (about 1,000 tokens, `Assumption: about 4 characters per token`). A file that fails is never truncated and never skipped: the run does not start, with one sentence naming the reason, the count and the file.
5. **Limit L40: only the person writes it.** The path rule calls it `other` under every spelling a disk opens as the file (`path_rule.fold`: Unicode case folding and compatibility forms; the review of this package found `inſtructions.md` creating the file on APFS, and the same hole for the flows folder and the configuration, which are closed too), and `returning` checks the inode and the real path the disk will write (a folder link on the way counts, the file there or not), keeps a name inside a protected file's name out, and validates every path before it writes any, so no run returns it, the mirror never sends it and no change set carries it; it never enters a copy as a file; the only writer in code is `ops_instructions`, through `INSTRUCTIONS_CHANNELS` (the terminal and the page), by default and whatever the channel policies of today grant (the maintainer's decision of 2026-10-16: every channel restriction becomes a policy with a restrictive default the person may widen; a later package of policies turns this default into a grant), with a compare-and-set on the file's sha256. Why not messaging or MCP: the file steers every later run, the unattended ones included, and messaging is the channel least bound to the person in front of the screen, the reasoning of `EFFECT_CHANNELS` and L33.
6. **Operations.** `instructions` (a read), `instructions-set` and `instructions-add` (the group `instructions`, no model call, an atomic write, a base hash: a file changed by hand since it was read is never overwritten). Routes `GET`/`POST /projects/{p}/instructions` and `POST .../instructions/add`; the Building's panel has an "Instructions" section.
7. **The assist proposes and never writes.** After the person's answer to an open question the reply may end with one proposal, offered by code with no model and no word list, only when the line is the person's own stored text (never a reply of a model or any external content), one line of 20 to 300 characters, passes the checks together with the file, is not already a line of it, and no proposal was made for the request (a cursor of the store). An answer through the page's inbox or a messaging reply proposes nothing, because those channels do not show the result (the request's one proposal is spent only where it is shown). On MCP the line was typed by an AI client. The 20-character floor is a choice of this package, so that "yes" or "the second one" is not offered as a rule. It is offered on every channel and accepted only from the terminal or the page. The `answer` row now calls `answer_proposing` and takes the channel, so that the text can tell messaging where to accept it.
8. **Routines.** The approved content gets `"instructions": <sha256>` only when the file exists; `CONTENT_VERSION` stays 1, so every routine of a project without the file keeps its approved hash (no re-approval, and no loss of safety: "absent" stays a value the hash distinguishes). A fire whose instructions changed stops and asks, naming "the project's instructions"; a task a fire made checks the file again at its run start and does not start when it differs from the approved one (it fails with the sentence "approve the routine again"; the next fire asks). Manual plans and standing policies are not changed.
9. **`AGENTS.md` stays separate.** It holds the project's technical rules and enters under L5; the assist never proposes a change to it.

**What was not done, on purpose.** No store column, no migration; no skill and no lab file edited, so no band changed and no evidence was written; the field evidence of a use does not record the presence of instructions (backlog INSTRUCTIONS); the first runtime is untouched. A contained run's prompt with a file is the measured text plus the block, not the measured text alone.

## 2026-10-16: a local model is tested in the container, behind a model server of its own (N14)

Package N14 of the delegation platform plan (D18 stands: a person's own tests stay local, and adding a model changes no fingerprinted file for a hosted model). The maintainer's decision of 2026-10-15: build now the way to test skills on local open-weight models, to try several and see whether any can run a skill; skill tests are development work, so slow CPU inference is acceptable. `evals/README.md`, "Testing a local model", is the procedure; `docs/architecture/platform/lab.md`, "A local model's server per run", the design.

- **The server is llama.cpp's, official image, pinned by digest (decision 1; principle 14).** The weights are one GGUF file, so one checksum and one read-only mount; the context (`-c`) and the chat template engine (`--jinja`, which tool calls need) are explicit flags, where Ollama silently keeps a small default context (the lesson of the adapter's README) and holds its weights in a writable store addressed by name; CPU images exist for `linux/arm64` and `linux/amd64` and a CUDA image exists; vLLM is GPU-first. The pins live in `evals/local-model-server.json`, outside the fingerprint: `ghcr.io/ggml-org/llama.cpp:server-b11277` (built 2026-09-30, 15 days before the pin; llama.cpp commit eae11d2217fe9225d1aaba48773b6cca45ae4de9) by its index digest, with the manifest digest of each CPU platform, and the CUDA image of the same build; source and access date are in the file. A test never pulls (`--pull=never`); `python3 evals/executor.py pull-local-server` pulls by digest and checks the digest docker lists. Docker's model runner (the only route to the Apple GPU) is not built: it is a host route (`model-runner.docker.internal`), which this design forbids; it is a separately verified option (backlog N14b).
- **One server per run, a sibling on the run's internal network (decision 2).** `wb-eval-model-<run id>` on `wb-eval-held-<run id>`, always internal, labelled like the key proxy's. Not one per event: the server keeps a prompt cache across requests, so a later run (a baseline) could be sped up by, or read, an earlier run's prompt, which holds the skill. The cost is a model load per run; the weights are memory-mapped, so a second load reads the page cache. On the 1.2 MB test model the server was healthy 2.2 s after it started, both times (5.3 s both times in a later run, under load); no larger model was loaded here, so the saving on a real one is not measured.
