The spec (`SPEC-EMAIL`, section 1) and its five recommendations were decided as written: IMAP and SMTP through the standard library; a dedicated mailbox for the bridge; a sender admitted only by the receiving server's own `Authentication-Results` (`dmarc=pass`; the DKIM fallback of the spec was dropped on the second review, below), failing closed when the server writes none; actions only in reply to the card; an e-mail messenger declares no attachments. The code is `providers/messenger/email-imap.py` (platform `email`, listen mode), `providers/_email_text.py` (the text helpers moved out of `providers/mailbox/gmail.py`, which binds the same names), the optional capability `actions_need_reply` of `runtime/messaging_provider.py` and the one reply check of `runtime/messaging_bridge.py` (`Bridge.replied_card`).

- **How the whole text travels, and how delivery is confirmed (the safe choice).** POL-1 (decision M-40) lets a messaging card approve an effect only when the card carried the whole text and, for a file, the provider confirmed that the file arrived (`Sent.document_failed`). An SMTP `250` is acceptance by the next server, not delivery, and e-mail has no trustworthy per-message receipt, so the document-first rule could never be satisfied for an attachment. The choice: the e-mail messenger declares `documents` false and `max_text` 8000 (cut to 8000 on a second review: a mail client clips a long message, and the lines that say how to act now come right under the first line), so the whole text is in the **body** of one plain-text message, or the card is cut (`messaging_cards.build` then rebuilds it with the command and "Too long to approve here") and is never approvable there; and the approval is taken only from a **reply that names the card's random Message-ID**, bound to the card replied to. A card that never reached the mailbox cannot be replied to, so it cannot be approved; the reply is the confirmation of delivery, which no send result is. The ledger's `whole` is therefore true for a card sent in one message, and what makes it usable is `actions_need_reply`, not the SMTP answer. A message with an attachment, and a card whose text does not fit, are approved on the page or in the terminal, as before.
- **Alternatives rejected.** (1) The whole text as an attachment, sent first: an attachment's arrival and display cannot be confirmed, so it would always end in a card with no approval, plus a useless first message. (2) E-mail cards never approvable for gated kinds: safe, but it takes away the one use the pilot has for the channel (a secretary approving a client e-mail from a phone), and it adds nothing the reply rule does not already give. (3) Trusting the From address, or SPF alone: an address is trivial to forge.
- **What stays code.** A sender is admitted only by `authenticated(topmost_results(...))`; a message that is not private, is automatic, is over 1 MiB, or whose sender is over 30 an hour is dropped with one log line by class and no reply (no backscatter, and no address, subject or text in a log); only the sender's own words reach `say` (quoted history, hidden HTML text and a signature never); the mailbox is only examined and peeked, never changed; TLS before any password; no header is built from a caller's text and a card goes to one address only. L45 of `contracts/runtime.md`.
- **Residual risks, written down.** A server that writes the authentication header for some messages only cannot be told from a forger who copies it (`--check` looks at the newest messages and exits 3 when none carries one); the mailbox account itself; a domain whose mail does not pass DMARC is dropped (safe, and the person sees it in the log class `unauthenticated`); a signed message captured by a third party and sent again can at most repeat what its sender approved, for a decision still open and unchanged. Nothing was run against a real mail host: the maintainer's live-verification steps are in the provider's README ("To verify on first use") and in the pull request.
- **The file is `email-imap.py`, not `email.py`.** The spec named `providers/messenger/email.py`. A script's own folder is first on its `sys.path`, so a file with that name is imported instead of the standard library's `email` package by every other script run from the folder: the first full run of the suite showed `whatsapp-cloud.py` failing at `import http.client`, and `telegram.py`, `discord.py` and the others would fail the same way when run as the bridge runs them (their tests load them by path and do not see it). A hyphenated stem cannot be imported, so it cannot shadow anything; it also leaves room for a Gmail API implementation of the same platform (`email-gmail.py`). A test fails if any provider script is named like a standard library module.
- **Beyond the spec.** A command is read from the first line alone (`/approve 12`), because the bridge reads whatever follows the number as a typed hash and a phone adds a signature; and the sender's own address is checked against the mailbox's (`self`), so the channel cannot answer itself. The Python 3.9 CI job lists provider test files by name; the channel's tests and `providers/mailbox/tests` were added to it on the second review.
- **After the independent review.** A reviewer who had only the code found no input that makes a forged message act; it found these, now fixed: the HTML parser of some Python releases takes quadratic time on unclosed tags, so a stranger who authenticates for their own domain could hang the one reader (the text of an HTML part is now made by a child process with a 3 s budget, a part over 200,000 characters is not tried, and the cheap checks, the address-header sizes and the authentication, run before anything expensive); `dmarc=pass` without `header.from`, a second DMARC result, a stray `)` and junk before a result no longer admit, and one parser reads the authserv-id; `--check` and `setup` look at the topmost header of every one of the newest messages; a failed fetch or a busy server no longer moves the cursor or stops the bridge (only a refusal of the credentials is exit 3); the uid search is made a window at a time; `/answer` needs the reply too; the invisible marks of tag characters and variation selectors are removed; the commands that stand alone are the bridge's own. Left as they are, and written in the README: a same-domain account can pose as a listed address for what is not reply-bound; hidden text by other styles than `display:none`; bottom-posted replies are dropped as empty; a `reject` or `release` is bound to the decision's id, as it is for a button.
- **After the second review (PR #448).** A quote inside a comment of the Authentication-Results header is plain text under RFC 5322, so a quoted MAIL FROM echoed by the server could inject a `dkim=pass`: a header that holds more dkim or dmarc results than were parsed out of it is not read. Headers over 16 KiB, or a header block over 64 KiB, are dropped before any header is read (a 589 KB Content-Type took 24 s); the rate (30 an hour for a sender, 120 for a domain) is asked before the text, and so before the HTML child; a message with two headers of our id is refused; one uid that fails three starts is skipped with a log line; `X-Autoreply`, `X-Autorespond`, `List-Post` and a bounce from `MAILER-DAEMON` or `postmaster` are automatic; the HTML child has a CPU limit. Written down, not fixed in code: the card's Message-ID is a bearer secret (a reply-all, a forward, a shared mailbox), a server that strips the quotes from an echoed address is a residual risk checked by hand, a banner above the text breaks the first-line command, and a reply-all that keeps a Cc is dropped without an answer.
- **After the re-review (PR #448): the DKIM fallback is removed; decided by recommendation, revertible.** The count of method tokens was bypassed when a hostile address was echoed only inside a comment, and it refused legitimate comments (an ARC summary, `reason="dkim=ok"`). It is replaced by the RFC 5322 reading: a header with a double quote inside a comment is not read. Because the DKIM-only path (`dmarc=none` plus an aligned `dkim=pass`) rests on reading the whole header as the server wrote it, with no DMARC record to confirm the alignment, it is removed, which reverses the DKIM fallback of spec question 3: an update is admitted only with exactly one `dmarc=pass` aligned to the From domain. `--check` and `setup` report when the newest messages carry no DMARC result and say why the channel refuses them (`--check` exits 3 when none does). The cost is a domain with DKIM and no DMARC record: its mail is refused until the owner publishes a DMARC record (`p=none` is enough). To revert: restore the second branch of `authenticated`, which the git history of this file holds. Also changed: the body of a message is parsed only in the budgeted child (a part with a 60,000-parameter Content-Type took 22.5 s), the parent reading the headers only; the per-domain rate applies only to addresses the provider has never written to; a refused fetch is retried at every poll with the next messages read, skipped after three failures over an hour with an error event, and a lost connection is never counted; only the footer block of a card moves up, so the decision's own text stays byte for byte; oversized headers are logged as `size`.
- **Not done.** Starting an e-mail from a phone (EMAIL-ROUTE, backlog), files on cards, the Gmail API as a second implementation, IDLE (Python 3.14), threading of the bridge's own messages.

## 2026-10-17: the nits of the final review of EMAIL-CHANNEL (pkg/email-channel-nits)

- **The footer is handed over, not inferred.** `messaging_cards.build` adds `message["footer"]` (the lines `text` ends with) for a platform that declares `actions_need_reply`, and only for it; the e-mail provider moves up only those lines, and only when the text ends with exactly them. A decision whose last paragraph looks like a footer, on a card with none of its own, stays as it is (the reviewer's `round3.py`). The field is the only change to the cards.
- **A skip does not reach the person; the README now says so.** The provider writes an error event of kind `failed` when it skips a message after three failures over an hour, and the bridge's `error` handling only logs it ("the messenger provider reported an error"); it does not message the listed senders. A notice would have meant a new path in `messaging_bridge.py` while POL-2 rebases on it, so the README was corrected instead (revertible: a notice through the bridge's existing notice path is the follow-up).
- **The list of messages to try again is capped at 200** (the oldest is given up with a log line), the cursor is written once for each pass, and an unexpected exception on one of them is logged and the pass goes on. The README names the residual cases of the comment rule (an unquoted parenthesis from a HELO, a server that escapes `"` but not `)`, both only when the server wrote no DMARC result), and that a listed sender the bridge has never written to is under the domain budget until the first card.

## 2026-10-17: policies per person; any group a place did not have is a widening (POL-2)

The maintainer asked for different permission sets for different channels and, where the platform allows it, different people: the owner's phone with full access, an employee's with a part. The repository's wording: "A policy is a named set of permissions. A channel has a default policy, and on a messaging app each listed sender may have their own." The seven recommendations of the addendum (`SPEC-POLICIES`, "Addendum: policies per person", P1 to P7) were approved as written, and the review of POL-1 added one requirement: a group inside a channel's ceiling, added by an edited `runtime.json`, was accepted without `--widen`; the maintainer's rule is that every channel restriction is a policy with a restrictive default that the person may widen, so that gap closed in this package.

- **The schema.** `runtime.json` gains `policies` (a profile name, lowercase words with hyphens, at most 32 characters, never `default`, to a list of groups or to `{"groups": [...], "notices": "all" | "actionable"}`); `channels.<channel>` is a list of groups (as before) or a profile name, the channel's default; an entry of `messaging.senders.<platform>` is a plain id or `{"id", "policy"}`. A sender's profile **replaces** the channel's groups for that sender (P2: it can be narrower). A file with no `policies`, flat lists and plain ids resolves exactly as before (a test compares the resolved groups for every channel and sender). The one resolution is `operations.effective_groups(channel, cfg, sender)` (POL-1's seam), with the sender's key `<platform>:<id>`; the loaded policy mapping carries each profiled sender's groups under `operations.SENDERS_KEY`, so every caller that already passes the mapping keeps working, and a grant counts only from an explicit entry or profile. The loader refuses an unknown profile, a profile with an unknown group or both `read` and `read-limited`, a profile a channel or a sender cannot take (the sentence names the profile and the channel; the rule is one function, `operations.group_refusal`), a sender listed twice on a platform, an id that is not text without a space of at most 64 characters, an entry object with other keys, and more than 100 senders in all. Three limits are new for files that used no profile: a sender listed twice on a platform (it was removed silently before; the addendum asks for a refusal), an id of more than 64 characters or with a control character, a platform name of more than 32 characters; each refusal names what to fix.
- **Any group is a widening (the review's requirement).** A widening is a group a place (a channel or a listed sender) has in the file being accepted and did not effectively have in the one accepted before: the default for messaging, the ceiling for the page and the MCP mode. So `release`, `approve-plans`, `reject-cancel`, `brakes`, `retry`, `routine-edit` on messaging are widenings, as are a group added to a profile that is assigned, a sender moved to a profile that is not a subset of what they had, a new sender (an id the accepted file did not list: compared with nothing, profile or not, since an id added to an edited file is a person newly allowed to act and the default is that a person has no power until the owner widens it), and a channel's entry removed. `accept-config --widen` takes the sha256 of that list (P1), one `<place>:<group>` line each; `config` shows it with a text line for each group (`operations.GROUP_TEXT`, and `GRANT_TEXT` for a grant). The record of what the accepted file effectively gave is the store's cursor `config:effective` (a bit mask over `GROUPS` for each place, a sender only by a short hash of its key, so that it carries no readable number (the hash is no secret) and fits a cursor of 4 KiB; at most 100 senders). `read` holds `read-limited`, and the umbrella each effect word (`operations.IMPLIED`): one line, not two.
- **Existing projects confirm once.** A project accepted before the record has POL-1's cursor `config:grants` or nothing, and cannot say what its accepted file gave: the baseline is "unknown" (only those grants were confirmed), `config` lists every group an entry has beyond them (the independent review found that reading the missing record as the defaults would let a page or MCP entry be widened unseen after the upgrade, and a second review that an absent entry and a mode change still ended the unknown state with nothing confirmed: every channel is compared, absent entry or not, and only `accept-config` writes the record), and the person runs `accept-config --sha256 <hash> --widen <the hash config shows>` once. The file stays in force meanwhile: it was accepted by the person, and a record that was never written cannot take it away. The alternative, treating the already accepted file as the baseline silently, would have left the gap open for every project that exists; the cost is one command.
- **Who acted (A2, A4).** The sender is the one the provider authenticated; the bridge asks the policy for that sender at every tap and line, when they act (the settings are read at each call), draws each card for its sender, refuses a card whose ledger `chat` is not the acting sender's, and passes the sender key to the operation, which asks again (`ops_actor.guard`) and records the actor. The store gets an `actor` column on `pending_decisions` and on `approvals` (migration 17, the two tables the addendum names and no other; the approvals trigger refuses to change it): `terminal`, `page`, `mcp`, `chat` or `messaging:<platform>:<profile>:<sender id>`. The existing `resolved_by` and `approved_by` keep the kind of actor they had, because other readers (progress, the page) read them and a phone number must not travel through them. The state file's generated approval row says "approved on telegram, profile owner", a cancel's label "user from messaging, profile owner"; `pending`, `task` and `progress` show `acted_by` ("owner on telegram"), never the id. A retry has no row to carry an actor (the task row has no such column, and adding one would touch a third table): it is checked for the sender and recorded nowhere. The id stays on old rows after an entry is removed.
- **What a profile sees (A5, P3).** `read-limited` is `read` without `costs`, `agents`, `connections` and `config`, and, going one step past the addendum, without `task` and `progress`, because both carry a run's cost; it is valid on messaging only. `notices: actionable` sends a card only for a decision the profile's card can act on or answer. A sender whose profile permits no read is sent no card.
- **What stays code.** The card in its own chat; a profile named by nobody but `accept-config` in the terminal (no operation has a profile or policy argument); the brakes; POL-1's rules per sender (the whole card, the hash, no approval from a typed line); a decision several senders received is resolved by the first act. **Not in the core (P5, P6):** per-person policies on the page and the MCP mode need sign-in, which belongs to the hosting project (the profile model is ready for it: `channels.page: "owner"` works today); recycled numbers and shared phones are documented, and an `until` date on a sender entry waits for a case. The WhatsApp linked device already resolves a hidden address to the phone digits and drops what it cannot resolve (`providers/messenger/whatsapp/inbound.go`).
- **Cost of the safety.** A person who adds a group or moves a sender types the hash of a list they read; a first acceptance of a file that lists more than the defaults asks for it too. No skill, no agent and no fingerprinted file changed: no bump and no lab run.
- **After the second review of the pull request** (decided by the maintainer's recommendation while away): an unknown baseline lists every channel and every sender (M1); a new sender is compared with nothing, so adding a person lists their groups once and needs `--widen` (M2: a restrictive default that the person widens by choice); a call from a messaging app must name its sender, in the bridge and in the operations layer, so the channel's wider set never answers for a sender with a narrower profile, and an operation that cannot be told the sender is not done from a messaging app (m1); a decision typed by id (`/reject`, `/release`, `/answer`) needs the sender's own card as an approval does, while `cancel`, `stop-task` and `retry` stay by id (m2); the same entry twice is one entry as before and only two different entries for one id are refused, an id may be 254 characters (an e-mail address), and the bridge's log says which refusal of the configuration stopped it, with no id (m3); a plain line about the state is answered only to a profile that reads, `/pending` lists a profile with actionable notices only what it can act on, and the actor of a command typed on the page or through the MCP mode is `page` or `mcp` (m5, nits). The short hash under which a sender is recorded is documented as no secret. After the third review: an unknown baseline compares every SENDER with nothing too (a new id added in the first edit after the upgrade would otherwise inherit the grants POL-1 confirmed for the channel); `status` names the configuration's file and hash and never its path (a staff phone must not receive a home folder through a third-party platform), `config` keeps the path, and one outgoing filter at the bridge's single send point replaces the home folder with `~` in every reply, card and file (commands in them stay runnable; the first claim, "no reply carries an absolute path", was wider than the code and is replaced by what the filter and its test cover); the NO_CARD reply is neutral ("Act on the card sent to you (send /pending).") since it answers reject, release and answer as well. There is one conversation for a project: a staff member's line can answer the router's open question for the owner's request; the plan still waits for a profile that approves it.

## 2026-10-17: a skipped e-mail reaches the person (F2-A)

The follow-up recorded under "the nits of the final review of EMAIL-CHANNEL" ("a notice through the bridge's existing notice path is the follow-up") is built, and that follow-up is closed. Decided by the spec's recommendations; the maintainer may revise.

- **A new error-event kind, `skipped`, not `failed`.** `failed` is written by Discord on every Gateway retry and by the WhatsApp providers on a lost connection and on rate limits, so a notice keyed on it would message the person about a retry. `skipped` means "the provider gave up a message the person may have sent; nothing was done with it". The listen session only counts it (`take_unread`); it does not set the run's kind or reason, so an exit 2 or 3 after it still stops the bridge with its own reason, and the reason is never stored, logged or forwarded.
- **The e-mail provider writes it** after three failures over an hour (as before, now `skipped`) and when the oldest of the 200 messages to try again is given up. An unexpected exception on one message stays a log line with no event: a stranger who passes DMARC for their own domain can craft a message that raises, and that must never become a message to the person (the no-backscatter rule).
- **A1, who is told: the recipients of the "N more waiting" line** (`hears_count`: the profile permits `pending` and is not `notices: actionable`), read-limited included, because no messaging operation expresses channel health and the lost message may be that sender's own. **A2, how often: at most one line per chat per hour** (`UNREAD_EVERY` = 3600 s; the provider itself needs an hour of retries before a skip), the count carrying the rest. **A3, persistence: none.** The untold counts live in the bridge's memory; a restart loses them and the provider's log line stays (a store row would have meant a migration while ATT-1 adds one).
- **The sentence is the bridge's own** (`UNREAD_ONE`, `UNREAD_MANY`): never the provider's reason, a uid, a Message-ID (a bearer secret, see "Written down, not fixed in code"), an address, a subject or a path; it passes `deliver`, so the home-folder filter applies anyway. Recipients are fixed when the skip is taken: a sender added later is not told of an earlier skip.
- **After the review of the pull request** (decided by the supervisor, reversible). The policy is read again when the line goes out: `Bridge.hearers()` is built first, the count is taken after it, and a chat that no longer hears loses what it was owed (the recipients were first fixed when the skip was taken, so a sender removed or narrowed during the hour would still have got it). A failed send keeps the count and is tried again an hour later, never at every tick (it first dropped the notice). A skip can be counted before the sender is known (a refused FETCH, the give-up of the oldest of 200), so whoever can make the server refuse a FETCH can cause at most one fixed line an hour per chat, carrying nothing of theirs; the README says so. The wording no longer says "after several tries" (a give-up can drop a message tried once), and a burst of `skipped` events is one log line.
- **Cost of the safety.** One more error kind that a provider may write; a bridge that reads a provider's event only as a number. No skill, no agent and no fingerprinted file changed: no bump and no lab run.

## 2026-10-17: a community full test counts only with 2 contributors (F2-B)

The maintainer approved the rule on 2026-10-17: a community full test also needs lines from at least 2 different contributors, none above two thirds, before it moves the community column. No lab cost, no fingerprinted file, no skill change.

- **The rule.** In the community source the pooled route is always on and a full test counts only through the pool (`community_rule` in `evals/eval_status.py`, the one predicate, true when the pool rule carries `who`, the fork owner of each test, which P11 already used). One fork's full test fills at most floor(`runs` x 2/3) of the counted lines of each case, so it never closes a pool alone. The newest community full test never decides the gate; the newest closed pool does, and a pair with no closed pool is `needs a test` with the kind `no passing full test` and the cause "no closed community pool" plus the pool's progress. Only a closed pool restarts the count of Y changes in this source. The command stays `eval_run.py --quick` (the existing hint of an open pool). The lab and the local sources are gated as before (a test pins it).
- **Why.** One fork's single full test made a pair `reliable` (`community_only`), which put one account's word, honest or not, over a ranking; the pool already needed two contributors for quick tests, and a full test is the same kind of line.
- **The current set needs two contributors too (option b, chosen by the maintainer after the review).** Once a pool had closed, a Y change or an epoch sent the pair to `watch` (`no current run`), and one fork's lines in the new current set cleared it, returning the pair to `reliable` while the new pool was open with one contributor. In the community source that watch cause now clears only when the current set's with-skill lines come from at least 2 contributors, none above two thirds, counted by the pool's own rule (`count_by_contributor`, shared by `pool_of` and `current_contributors`, so no second copy): **per case**, a contributor's lines count up to floor(`runs` x 2/3) of the case's first 3, then the contributors and the largest share are read over the counted lines. Skipped when the pool of the current `X.Y` has closed (it has satisfied the same rule). The cause reads "the current runs of X.Y come from 1 of 2 contributors, ..." with the quick-test command. The Y-changes cause in this source reads "since the newest closed pool", with the same command. Lab and local sources are unchanged (a test pins it).
- **What it gives up.** A single honest contributor can no longer rank a pair with one full test, nor bring it back from `watch` after a Y change or an epoch. What it gains: no one fork ranks a pair by itself.
- **Status on the committed evidence is unchanged** (no community evidence file is committed): the output of `python3 evals/eval_status.py status` is byte-identical before and after.
- **Tests.** New `evals/tests/test_community_full_test_contributors.py`; the tests of `evals/tests/test_community_evidence.py` that asserted one fork's full test makes a pair reliable were rewritten with a second fork, keeping their intent.
- **Which cases count (decided by the supervisor, reversible).** The contributors and the two-thirds share of the current set are counted over the cases that ran in it, as a lab partial test would cover them: two contributors on one changed case can clear `no current run` (the score floor still applies). The check can also turn a pair back to `watch` when one contributor adds lines to cases no one else ran; it only moves the safe way.
- **Open questions, resolved by recommendation (the maintainer may revise).** B1: `AGENTS.md` is not edited, no sentence there states the opposite. B2: the status hint for an open community pool stays the existing quick-test command.

## 2026-10-17: a scheduled job keeps the interpreter it was approved with (F2-C)

A scheduled post hung because a new Python, managed by the package runner, had been installed the day before: the runner picks an interpreter at each start, every scheduled provider that reads the macOS keychain is started that way, and the keychain trusts a binary, not a version, so the first firing on the new binary raised a prompt nobody could answer. The scheduler's own layer was already pinned (launchd starts the runner with the system interpreter, hashed in the approval); the moving part was below it.

- **What was built.** `schedule` (both providers) reads `UV_PYTHON` from its own environment. An absolute path to an executable file is resolved to the real binary and recorded in the job as `python` (`path`, `sha256`); the approval digest covers it only when it is present, so a job planned without it keeps the digest it had (a fixed digest is tested); the confirmed call hashes it again; every firing checks it and refuses a changed, missing or unreadable interpreter (the same now holds for the program: an unreadable file is a refusal, not a crash) (a one-shot job is recorded `refused`, a recurring one refused and unloaded, with the scheduler's notification: a loud refusal replaces a silent hang); the command starts with `UV_PYTHON` set to the recorded path, so every `uv run` in its process tree uses it. The tick never calls `schedule`: the one runtime caller is the vote approval (`approve --confirmed`, `vote_approve`), which hands the scheduler its own environment, so the vote post is pinned only when `UV_PYTHON` is set in the environment of the `approve` command (or of the service that runs it); `approve` now carries the dry run's `warnings` into its result, so the person sees that the post was scheduled unpinned. `UV_PYTHON` also reaches everything a pinned job starts (the dispatcher's work in other projects), and overrides their `.python-version`. `providers/resolve.py` keeps `UV_PYTHON` in `ENV_MINIMUM` (it names an interpreter, never a value), so a provider started through the resolver keeps the pin. No skill and no fingerprinted file changed.
- **Decided by recommendation (the maintainer may revise).** C1: no automatic pin when `UV_PYTHON` is unset, the dry run only warns, because the choice uv makes at creation is the thing that moves and a pin the person did not choose may not be the binary the keychain trusts. C2: a changed interpreter at a firing refuses the job rather than running it unpinned (approval-bound, like the program and the runner). C3: no persistent setting, the environment only (it is uv's own variable, it propagates to jobs scheduled from inside a pinned job and needs no new state). C4: reading the keychain through Apple's signed `/usr/bin/security`, so that no interpreter matters, is recorded as the alternative and not built; it is larger and security-sensitive (the resolver, every item's access list).
- **Cost.** Each existing job is re-created once with the variable set, because a job has no migration (a job without `python` runs as before, unpinned); the pin is the person's choice, and an upgrade or removal of the pinned interpreter stops the job at its next firing until it is scheduled again with the new path, after the keychain trusts it. A job's runner copy and a tick's snapshot of `providers/resolve.py` change only when the job is re-created.

## 2026-10-17: OneDrive files as sources: the permission `Files.Read`, and one write to the secret store (O1)

The maintainer approved two answers of the OneDrive plan (`SPEC-ONEDRIVE`, "Open questions"): the provider may write the refreshed token back into the OS secret store (Q-A), and the permission is the delegated `Files.Read` with `offline_access` and nothing else (Q-B). The provider is `providers/documents/onedrive.py` with `onedrive_auth.py`; the runtime side (the source type, the migration, the terminal command) is the next package.

- **The permission.** Microsoft has no permission granted per chosen file on both account kinds. `Files.Read` is the least privileged one that downloads and searches, needs no admin consent and exists for personal accounts; it lets the token read every file of the person's own OneDrive, and writes none. The README and `--help` say so in those words, as they did for `drive.file`. The narrowing to chosen files is the runtime's. Rejected: `Files.Read.All` (also SharePoint and shared files; excluded from user consent by Microsoft's default policy, so an admin is needed; it waits for a real case, package O3), `Files.Read.Selected` (preview, work and school only, access for hours), `Files.SelectedOperations.Selected` (admin consent and a grant per file by a site owner), `Files.ReadWrite.AppFolder` (the app's own folder; the scope of a later write-back, in a separate secret). A returned scope other than `Files.Read` (the four that grant no file access aside) is refused with exit 3 and the extra scope named.
- **The exception to rule 1 of `contracts/secrets.md`.** Microsoft replaces the refresh token on every use. After a refresh the provider writes the new token into the one record it found in the store (same service and username), only when the record was found in the store; when the write fails it carries on with the old token and says so on stderr. A record from the environment or the credentials directory is never rewritten. Without it a person would give consent again every 90 days. `onedrive_auth.py forget` deletes the record, our own local entry. The tests use a stand-in store; no test reads the real one.
- **By recommendation, reversible.** One multi-tenant registration (the maintainer's) by default, an office's own single-tenant registration allowed through the record's `tenant` and `client_id` (Q2); SharePoint and shared files are not in this package (Q3); a legacy `.doc`, `.xls` or `.ppt` is refused with "save it as .docx, .xlsx or .pptx", not converted remotely (Q4); a OneDrive search box on a page waits for the sources screen (Q5). There is no Microsoft picker: it needs SharePoint tokens, narrows nothing under `Files.Read`, and the page loads nothing from another host; the person chooses in the terminal with `search` and `children`.
- **Not run against Microsoft.** Everything is tested against a stand-in service; the README's "To verify" lists what the live step must confirm (the download hosts, the scope string, `localhost` reaching the listener, a work tenant's consent, the rotation).

## 2026-10-17: the Google calendar provider and the Gmail additions (G1 of the secretary integrations)

The person's mail and calendar become sources (dated, scanned snapshots) in a later package of the spec; this one builds the provider side that needs no runtime file: a read-only Google Calendar provider and two additions to the Gmail reader. The maintainer approved the spec's recommendations on 2026-10-17 (questions 3 and 9); the scope choice is recorded here.

- **The Google Calendar scopes are `calendar.events.readonly` and `calendar.calendarlist.readonly`, and nothing else.** Together they read every event of every calendar the account sees and the list of calendars, and change nothing. They are narrower than `calendar.readonly` (which also reads the properties of the calendars). `calendar.events.owned.readonly` is narrower still and drops the calendars shared with the person; it was not chosen because a shared family or team calendar is a normal thing to read, and the narrowing to one calendar and one window is the runtime's job anyway. **Cost of the safety:** two scopes to consent to instead of one wide one, and a consent screen where the person must keep both boxes ticked (with one unticked nothing is stored).
- **The provider refuses any token whose scope is not exactly those two (exit 3).** Unlike the Gmail reader, which only warns about an extra scope, a wider token, a narrower one or one that reports no scope is a setup error to fix. **Cost:** a token that Google reports without a scope field cannot be used until it is shown to be the narrow one (the Drive provider's rule).
- **The Desktop app client of Gmail may be reused, with a separate stored record.** Google does not support incremental authorization for installed apps, so a token carries the scopes of its own consent: the calendar record (`calendar-google`, the secret `GOOGLE_CALENDAR_TOKEN`) asks only for the two calendar scopes and cannot read mail. The client travels with the token in the record (the Drive provider's shape), so there is one entry and no second client secret in the store. That a calendar token cannot be made to carry a Gmail scope is documented behaviour and is still to be measured live. **Cost:** one more record, and one more consent when the Gmail one is redone.
- **Read only, GET only.** No verb changes a calendar. The calendar write (a new effect word, its own grant) stays unbuilt until a real case asks.
- **A time without an offset is a usage error.** The service refuses one, and the provider does not guess the local zone; the runtime passes the offset from the host's clock and zone.
- **Only the fields a snapshot needs are asked of the service.** No description, no attachment, no attendee address and no conference link is asked for. The organizer's address, the person's own invitation answer and the conference id are received only to derive `organizer_domain`, `response` and `online_meeting`, and are never printed; the organizer is a domain, never an address. Titles and places are free text and may hold a URL, which the snapshot (RT-1) removes. A token the service hands out is accepted only as printable ASCII without space, a header the library refuses names only the kind of the failure, and an unexpected failure prints only its kind, so no traceback can hold a token.
- **Gmail: `--own-words` and `attachments`.** `--own-words` cuts the quoted history of a reply (`strip_quoted`, moved unchanged from the e-mail channel to `providers/_email_text.py`, which both load). `attachments` lists name, declared type and a size worked out from the encoded length: no part is decoded or opened (a test fails on any decode of an attachment part), an inline part is not listed, at most 50 are listed. `--own-words` is also offered on `read-eml`, beyond the spec's `search` and `get`, so that it can be tried and tested offline.
- **No skill, no agent and no fingerprinted file changed: no bump and no lab run.** No call to Google was made; the endpoints are those the spec writer read on 2026-10-17 and the provider was tested against a stand-in service. The items still to verify live are listed in `providers/calendar/README.md`.

## 2026-10-17: a file sent through a messaging app is held and added by a person (ATT-1, runtime part)

The maintainer approved the spec's recommendations for the open questions of `SPEC-ATTACH` (nothing downloaded with the group off; `add-sources-direct` needs `--widen`; limits fixed in code; macro-enabled files refused for every source; only an exact `@<id>` caption is read; images, voice notes and video refused until there is OCR or transcription; an attachment version usable unattended like a page upload; handing a held file to a request that waits for its plan later). This is the runtime half (`pkg/attach-runtime`, ATT-1a); the messaging half (the update, the fetch, the card) is ATT-1b. Migration 18; limit L48 (the hold); L47 (the fetch) comes with ATT-1b.

- **Where the checks live.** What the platform declared (a medium, a size over the cap), the day's count and the held ceiling are checked by the bridge before a byte is fetched (`attachments.precheck`, from the `attachments` read), and checked again by `attachment-hold` after the bytes arrived (the operation is the authority: a bridge that skips them, or two fetches that race, still meet the limit, and the refused file is recorded and counts against the day). The type is read from the bytes by the intake; a declared type of the converter's list that the bytes contradict is refused (`mismatch`), a generic or absent one goes by the bytes.
- **`attachment-hold` is the bridge's alone** and says so in code: it refuses any channel but messaging, asks the sender's profile (`ops_actor.guard`), and checks the grant for a direct add itself (`operations.granted(..., "direct", sender)`). `policy_allows` with the kind `direct` would also be true for a profile that only lists `add-sources`, because a group with no kind covers every kind; the grant is therefore read through `granted`, which counts only the groups of `GRANTS`. The grant `add-sources-direct` holds the one call `attachment-hold` (as the spec has it); it does not hold the reads or the decisions, so the bridge reads the held list and the day's count itself (as it reads `pending`), outside the policy, to make its checks.
- **The sender's id is in the store alone.** The table `attachments` keeps it (and the platform's reference only as a hash); a shell's row, a version's row, its manifest and its catalog line carry the platform and the profile name. `sources.clean_provenance` admits four keys, words of lower-case letters, digits and hyphens, and a number: a sender id or a phone number is refused by its shape. The row is never deleted (L13), so the id stays on an old row after the sender's entry is removed, as POL-2 A4 says of the actor.
- **Add: delete whatever the result, with one exception.** The held file is deleted when it is added, refused, discarded or expired. A problem of this machine that is not the file's (the sources folder is not usable, another host holds its lease, the converter is not set up: the refusals that are not codes of the file) leaves the file held, so that it can be added once that is mended; deleting it would lose the file for a reason that is not its own. A reply names a fixed reason for a refusal that is the file's (`it holds what looks like a credential`, `it holds macros, which are never converted`, ...) and never the converter's own text, because that text can carry words of the file.
- **A target that changed.** A source id taken from the name is made so that no source and no other held file has it. If the source named at hold time has changed state when the file is added (a free id that became a source, a source that gained its first version), the add is refused and the file stays held, with the sentence that says to discard it and send it again: a held file never becomes a version of a source it was not shown against.
- **Expiry does not wait for the upkeep.** The pass of `upkeep` (once an hour, in the dispatcher's round) deletes a file held 7 days and removes the leftovers of a crash; `attachment-add` also refuses a file past its week (and deletes it), so a project whose dispatcher does not run cannot add what has expired.
- **The page.** A section of the Building's panel lists the held files and decides them; the page had no sources screen before, and this section is only for the files. It reads when the page reloads (a held file arrives with a write of the store), so the Building's reads on a reload gain one: `GET /attachments`.
- **ATT-1b: where the messaging half sits.** `runtime/messaging_attach.py` holds the whole of it and the bridge dispatches to it in three places (an `attachment` intent, a tap on a card of the kind `attachment`, and `/add` and `/discard`); the provider keys, the fetch and the listen op are in `messaging_provider.py`. The ledger's card of a file has `pending_id` null and an `attachment_id`, so no lookup by a decision's number meets it. Limit L47 is the fetch.
- **A one-shot provider says `gone` on stderr.** *(Superseded by the review of #458 below: the rule is now exit 1 and the last non-empty line of stderr exactly `gone`; "the word `gone` first on stderr" is no longer read.)* The resolver gives the bridge the stderr of a failed verb and not its stdout, so the spec's `\"gone\": true` cannot reach it: a provider that finds a reference no longer served exits 1 with the word `gone` first on stderr; a listen provider answers `kind: gone`. A fetch answer in the listen protocol is read by the op that was asked (the waiting entry remembers it), so a send answer cannot pass for one.
- **The bridge does not take the provider's word.** After a fetch the bridge looks at the folder: one regular file named `content`, of the size the provider answered, and no larger than what was allowed; anything else is refused and the folder removed. The day's bytes left bound the fetch, and `attachment-hold` bounds it again.
- **Nothing is fetched while the policy says no, and the page's list is outside the policy.** The bridge reads the held list and the day's count itself (as it reads `pending`) for its checks; what it does for the sender (fetch, hold, add, discard) is always asked of the policy for that sender. `/pending` lists a sender's files only when their profile permits `attachments`.
- **After the review of the messaging half (#458).** (1) */pending names only the sender's own files that have a card in their chat*, and a typed `/add n` or `/discard n` binds to the newest card of that file in that chat, as before. (2) *Every fetch counts*: the bridge reports a fetch that ended with no file (`failed`, with the bytes it actually wrote, 0 for a transient error: the file counts for one, so a flood stops at the file count and a hiccup does not cost the day's bytes), before the fetch lock is released, so oversized or vanished files stop at the day's limit; a same reference twice in one message is fetched once; a busy lock stops the rest of the message. (3) *A platform's name and type are cleaned where the update is validated* (a name to 1,024 characters with control, format and separator characters out, a type to `type/subtype` of at most 127 characters or a word of the media kinds, else none). (4) *A phone gets no path*: an error of the operations layer that is a machine problem, or carries a path, is a fixed sentence and only its class is logged. (5) *`gone` has one rule*: exit 1 and the last non-empty line of stderr exactly `gone` (the resolver now keeps that line, `ProviderCallError.stderr_last`, because its tail has lost its line ends). (6) The provider's file is also hashed against the sha256 it answered and must have no other name; a negative `max_attachment_bytes` is refused. (7) The Twilio API-key row of `contracts/secrets.md` and the registry in `providers/secrets/resolver.py` say the same thing again.
- **No provider declares `attachments` yet.** Until ATT-2 to ATT-4 merge, every bridge answers \"This app's connection does not take files yet.\"; the live checks are the maintainer's (`docs/backlog.md`, AT1).
- **After the review of the runtime half (#456).** (1) *A sender decides only their own files.* On a messaging app `attachments` returns the acting sender's rows and `attachment-add` and `attachment-discard` answer \"No file n waits for you.\" for a file whose platform and sender are not theirs; `attachment-add` and `attachment-discard` leave the conversation's commands, so from a phone the only way to decide is the answer to the card of that file, in that chat, with its hash checked (the typed `/attachment-add 1` of another listed sender added the first sender's file). The terminal and the page keep the whole list (they are the person's own). (2) *The converter's refusal has its own exit.* `providers/converter/container.py` exits 4 when the program in the container exited 3 (it refused the content); `sources.provider_convert` gives that the code `convert` (the file's doing) and every other failure of the provider the code `converter` (the machine's), and an attachment is kept held for every machine failure instead of one matched by its message. (3) *The grant makes new sources.* A direct request for a source that exists, with or without a version, is held with a card; the grant text says so. (4) *Every fetch counts.* A fetch that ended with no file is reported to the hold (`failed`, `fetched_bytes`) and recorded as a refused row; a duplicate fetch, a page upload in progress and a declared medium or size found at the hold are recorded the same way; the intake is bound by the least of the file cap, the provider's cap and the day's bytes left; the provider's file must have one name. The direct add runs under the attachments lock. (5) *A phone gets no path.* A machine problem is a `MachineProblem` whose `phone` is a fixed sentence.
- **Cost of the safety.** A file from a phone costs the person one answer (a card, a click, or a typed command) unless they name the grant; the day's caps and the ceiling of 10 can refuse a file the person wanted until tomorrow or until they answer the others; a file refused for what it holds is deleted and must be sent again. No skill, no agent and no fingerprinted file changed: no bump and no lab run.

## 2026-10-17: the unofficial WhatsApp client takes files, and bounds a download where it arrives (ATT-4)

- **The library has no bounded download.** At the pinned whatsmeow version `Download` reads the whole response into memory; `DownloadToFile` exists, but nothing may be written before the hash is checked, so the bytes are held in memory anyway and counted by the media client. A declared length is the sender's word (an authenticated one, but written by the sender), so it only refuses early. The program therefore replaces the client the library downloads media with (`SetMediaHTTPClient`): HTTPS only, a 10-second connect, no redirect followed, and a response body that counts what arrives and cancels the download over the cap (plus 64 bytes for the encryption's padding and MAC). The cap travels in the fetch's context, so uploads and the library's own downloads are not limited by it. Memory is held to about the cap, not to the sender's claim.
- **A ceiling of 100 MiB, declared as `max_attachment_bytes`.** It is the most the program holds for one file because the library decrypts in memory; it is the program's ceiling and not a limit WhatsApp states. The bridge's cap (25 MiB) is lower, so nothing changes today; the number is kept equal in `whatsapp.py` and `whatsapp/fetch.go` by a test.
- **A reference is stable for a message, random for the world.** 32 random hex characters, never the media key or an address; held for one hour, at most 10 per sender and 200 in all (the list of senders is applied by the bridge, so any one-to-one chat reaches the table, and one sender must not push another's references out); the same message delivered again within the hour keeps the same reference, so the bridge's duplicate rule (same platform and reference) holds. A restart makes every earlier reference `gone`.
- **403, 404 and 410 from the media host are `gone`.** WhatsApp keeps a medium for a limited time and answers with one of the three when the address no longer serves it. Everything else that fails is `failed` with a sentence of the program's.
- **The folder is checked, not trusted.** The program writes only through a root opened on the folder the bridge made, after it checked the folder is a real directory (no link) of the user with no access for the group or others, and that the root is the directory it looked at. This depends on the bridge making the folder 0700, as the spec says.
- **Cost of the safety.** A download that would have worked through a redirect or a plain address fails; the library's default client has been the only one to download media so far, so the first live fetch is the check (`providers/messenger/README.md`, WhatsApp, Tests). No skill, no agent and no fingerprinted file changed: no bump and no lab run.

## 2026-10-17: files in the Telegram and Discord providers (ATT-2)

- **What is built.** `telegram.py` maps `document`, `photo`, `voice`, `video`, `audio` and `sticker` (and `video_note`, named `video`) to `attachments`, with the caption as `text` only beside a file, and serves `fetch-attachment`; `discord.py` maps `attachments[]` to a ref of three ASCII-digit ids and serves the listen op `fetch`. Both declare `attachments` and `max_attachment_bytes`.
- **The caps.** Telegram declares 20 MiB, the Bot API's download limit as documented (a `getFile` answer of "file is too big" is exit 2, not a failure). Discord states no limit for a bot, so it declares 25 MiB, the most the bridge takes for a source. Both come from the documented behaviour and were not re-read here (no network); the live test confirms them.
- **The fetch is bounded the same way on both.** The address is built or re-read, never taken from an update (Telegram: the fixed base and a closed pattern for the path; Discord: the message read again for a fresh signed address). Discord's content host is asked through a second opener with no `Authorization` header and no redirect. Bytes are counted while they stream (a declared size over the cap is refused before the first read), the file is written by descriptor inside the folder opened without following a link (`O_EXCL`, `O_NOFOLLOW`, mode 0600), a failed fetch removes the file it made (a process killed mid-write can leave a partial one, which the bridge removes with its folder), and `http.client.HTTPException` is caught where a body is read (a cut stream names only its class).
- **Discord fetches run on their own thread** so a download of up to 120 s never holds a send, as the contract says ("a fetch runs beside the sends").
- **Cost.** A video note arrives as a refused medium instead of being ignored in silence; a Discord id with a non-ASCII digit is not mapped. No skill, no agent and no fingerprinted file changed: no bump and no lab run.

## 2026-10-17: files from the two official WhatsApp providers (ATT-3)

`whatsapp-cloud` and `whatsapp-twilio` now declare `attachments` and serve the listen op `fetch` of `SPEC-ATTACH` section 5, on the contract ATT-1b built. What the package decided where the spec left a choice:

- **The fetch has a thread of its own.** The shared listener (`whatsapp_meta.py`) reads a `fetch` request off standard input into a second queue, served by a second thread, so a download of up to 120 seconds never holds up a send (the contract says a fetch "runs beside the sends"). One fetch runs at a time, as the bridge asks for one at a time per project.
- **One writer of the file, one reader of the folder.** Both providers write through `save_stream`: the folder opened by descriptor (absolute, not a link, owned by the user), `content` created with `O_EXCL|O_NOFOLLOW` mode 0600 through that descriptor, the cap enforced at the first byte over it, a declared length over the cap refused unread, a deadline for the whole fetch, the partial file removed on any failure. A name the platform declares is cleaned and shown, never part of a path.
- **A host list is an exact list, and the request is made to the rebuilt address.** `url_on_hosts` accepts an address only if it is printable ASCII with no backslash, https, no user information, the default port, and a host equal to one entry of the list; it then returns the address rebuilt from its parsed parts, so a parser that reads the original differently cannot connect somewhere else. Cloud: `lookaside.fbsbx.com`. Twilio: `media.twiliocdn.com` and `mms.twiliocdn.com`. Neither list was re-read from the platform's documentation for this change (no network): a host not on the list fails closed, and the README names the live check.
- **Cloud follows no redirect at all.** The request to the media host carries the token, so the opener raises on any redirect (the same handler as the Graph opener), where Twilio's contract follows exactly one, to its media host, without the key. The token and the key are each put into a header in one place of the source (a test already counted them).
- **A declared hash of no known shape is a failure.** Graph's `sha256` is accepted as hex or as base64 (Meta's documentation was not re-read for this change, and the form is not certain, so both are handled); anything else refuses the file instead of skipping the check. If the live test shows a third form, that is one line in `declared_digest`.
- **A message whose media cannot all be read is dropped whole (Twilio).** An update that listed only some of the files would hide the rest from the person, so a bad `MediaUrlN`, a count over 10 or a missing field drops the message with one log line by class, as an unreadable medium of the Cloud API does.
- **Twilio's ref is the strict pair `<MM...>:<ME...>` and the account is the settings'.** The address in an update is parsed by a full-match pattern and must name the configured account and carry the webhook's own `MessageSid`; the fetch never uses it, it rebuilds the address from the two ids.
- **A refused key on the media path fails the fetch and does not stop the provider.** A 401 or 403 on Twilio's media path is most often a key without the permission to read media, which would otherwise stop the whole provider (exit 3) over one file; a key really revoked is caught by the next send. The answer says the key may lack the permission.
- **The deadline is looked at after every receive.** The body is read with `read1` (`read(n)` waits for `n` bytes, which a host dripping a byte at a time stretches without limit); a loopback test with a real response proves it.
- **The one-shot verb `fetch-attachment` exists as a stub** that exits 2 with the listen-only sentence in both providers, as `receive`, `send` and `ack` do; the bridge never calls it on a listen provider.
- **Cost of the safety.** A medium Meta serves from a host the list does not name, or Twilio redirects somewhere else, is refused until the list is corrected after the live test; a hash in a form not yet seen refuses the file. Both fail closed and say which check refused. No skill, no agent and no fingerprinted file changed: no bump and no lab run.

## 2026-10-17: a question answered from the content of the sources (AQ-1)

The router sees only the catalog of the sources, and the full text reaches only a task of an approved plan, so a person who asks "what is the notice period in the lease?" gets a plan to approve, a second run and a delivery to read. Package AQ-1 of `SPEC-ANSWER-FROM-SOURCES` builds the runtime half: `/ask <question>` (and the terminal's `ask`, the page's `POST /projects/{p}/ask`) answers in one run, with the quotes it rests on. The maintainer approved the fourteen recommendations of the specification on 2026-10-17; this entry records what the code does with them.

- **Shape (c): `ask` runs one contained answer run, with no plan per question.** A question has no effect and writes nothing to the project, so a plan approval per question would protect nothing; the content goes only to the person who asked, whom the widening of the group `ask` already authorised. The alternative kept open is a request for a report, which is planned like any task. The contained run's core (`ops_contained.routed`, `execute`) now has two callers; `contained_run` is unchanged in behaviour and tests.
- **The sources are chosen by code** (`runtime/ask_select.py`): `@` mentions, else all that fit 256 KiB and five files, else a ranking of plain word rules over the catalog entries, ties to the newer version and then the id. A model choosing from the catalog was rejected: one more run per question, the catalog is third-party text that could steer which documents are read, and it cannot be tested offline. The 256 KiB, five sources, 300 seconds and 2,000 characters are the specification's choices, not measured.
- **The reply is checked by code** (`runtime/ask_reply.py`): each quote must be a substring of its source's copy, `not-found` replaces the model's prose with a fixed sentence, an id the run was not given is flagged, an instruction found in a source is kept in the reply and never followed. **Unproven is a label, not a gate:** the footer `Unproven: <skill> has no passing test on <model>` is added whenever the run's autonomy is not `proven`, because a reply has no review to hold; the strict alternative (refuse until the skill is reliable) would make nothing work until the battery.
- **Policy.** The row `ask` lists the terminal, the conversation (`/ask` is a typed line, which needs the channel `chat` in the row), the page and messaging, never the MCP mode. The group `ask` is in no default; a messaging sender gets it only from a profile or channel entry accepted with `--widen` (POL-2). The page keeps it in its ceiling unless its policy narrows it. A version the person changed by hand is given only to an ask typed in the terminal or the conversation's own shell, the rule of tasks.
- **Records without a migration.** `answer_run_start` is `route_run_start` with no web (so a router run and an answer run exclude each other, which is the "one conversation model call per project at a time" the specification asks for) and `answer_run_finish` ends the run and the request together. The actor is a cursor `ask-actor:<run id>`, because no column holds it and a migration was not worth it. An answer run counts against the owner of the answer skill through one helper (`ops_core.attributed`), shared by the planning agent's check and the day's use of every agent.
- **Limits L49 and L50** (numbered after the attachments' L47 and L48, which are in their own branch). **What did not change:** no skill, no case, no eval adapter, no fingerprinted file; no bump and no lab run. The skill that answers (`asst-answer`) waits for the maintainer's real questions (backlog AQ2); until it is written a real checkout replies that no skill answers yet.
- **Deviations from the specification, each with its reason.** The row also lists the channel `chat`: a typed `/ask` is read by `parse_chat`, which accepts only rows that list it. `ops_contained` exposes two helpers (`routed`, `execute`) rather than one: the second needs the copies made and removed by its caller, so that the reply can be checked against them. A quote over 300 characters is flagged (`QUOTE_TOO_LONG`) and not verified, so that a quote cannot carry a whole document. The hint after a direct reply also requires an answer skill to be configured, so that it never promises what `/ask` cannot do yet. No use of the skill is recorded for field evidence (the contained run records none): a later package can add it.
- **After the review of the pull request** (decided by the supervisor and the reviewer's findings). The conversation is a read that the MCP mode has, so the reply to a `/ask` is stored there with its answer run's id (the page redraws from the conversation and would lose it otherwise), and `conversation` is told the channel that reads it: it shows the reply only to the channel that asked (and on a messaging app only to that sender), and everyone else, the MCP mode always, gets "(answered from the sources: request <n>)"; the asker is the cursor `ask-actor:<run id>`, kept like the actor of a decision. A model's text must not pass for what code checked: code prints the verified quotes in a form only it writes (`Checked quote`), a quote needs 20 characters and 3 words, any other line that imitates the form (after NFKC, format characters removed, look-alike letters folded) is left out and flagged, and after the instructions line only a line that names a source, says `not followed` and quotes a passage that is in that source is kept. `ask` alone lets a profile ask (the bridge asks the group `ask`, not `talk`); the recovery (the next ask, a task's run, the dispatcher's round) cancels the question a dead process left; the title of a question is "Question <n>", with the text only in the request row; the policy is read again before the answer is returned; the board marks a question by its runs, not by the configuration now. **Third check:** what is shown of the model's text is cleaned of the format characters, the combining marks and the default-ignorable code points (a line with a text-direction control is left out), the look-alikes of Armenian, Cherokee and the Latin small capitals are folded, and a kept instruction line is written again by code (`[<id>] "<passage>" (not followed)`); the questions a dead ask left are found by the title code gave them ("Question <n>"), not by the name of the router's skill.
- **Cost of the safety.** One more operation that sends the text of the person's documents to a model and, on a messaging app, through the platform's servers; the group is off by default, per sender, and its sentence says so.

## 2026-10-18: the combining marks of other languages are shown, and a question is abandoned only by both marks (AQ-1 follow-up)

Two small corrections to the answer from sources (#460), found after it merged.

- **Combining marks stay in the shown text.** The cleaning of what an answer shows removed every combining mark (Mn) so that a mark could not hide the form code writes, and with it the vowel signs and the virama of Devanagari and the vowel marks of Thai, which have no precomposed letter: a Hindi greeting lost its vowel signs and its viramas and came out with its words broken. `clean()` now removes only the format characters (Cf) and the default-ignorable code points and keeps the rest; the imitation check, `fold()`, still drops every combining mark, so a mark cannot hide `Checked quote` (tests for Devanagari, Thai, Vietnamese and Portuguese, decomposed and precomposed, and for the imitation tests as before). A quote is compared with its source in NFC after the same cleaning on both sides.
- **An abandoned question needs both marks.** `answers_abandoned_cancel(conn, router_skill)` cancels a request only when code titled it "Question N" and it has no run of the router's skill (and has runs, none running, and no task). Either alone was a guess: a title can be typed by a person, and a router skill can be renamed.
- No skill, no case and no fingerprinted file changed: no bump and no lab run.

## 2026-10-17: the person's Outlook mail and calendar as sources: one Microsoft app registration per purpose (M1)

The maintainer approved the recommendations of `SPEC-SECRETARY-INTEGRATIONS` on 2026-10-17: one Entra registration per purpose (mail and calendar apart from OneDrive's files registration), the delegated permissions `Mail.Read` and `Calendars.ReadBasic` plus `offline_access`, read only, and the shared Graph token code moved into `providers/_msgraph.py`. The providers are `providers/mailbox/outlook.py` (the second implementation of `reader:email`) and `providers/calendar/outlook.py` (an implementation of the new class `integration:calendar`), each with an `outlook_auth.py`. The runtime side (the source types `mail` and `calendar`, the snapshot text, the freshness bound) is a later package (RT-1) and is not built here.

- **Why three registrations, not one.** Microsoft documents that a refresh token is bound to a user and a client and is not tied to a resource, so a client that held `Files.Read`, `Mail.Read` and `Calendars.ReadBasic` could use one refresh token for all three. The code is shared; the registration, the client id, the permission and the record are not: `ONEDRIVE_SOURCES_TOKEN`, `OUTLOOK_MAIL_TOKEN` and `OUTLOOK_CALENDAR_TOKEN` are three entries, and each provider refuses a token whose reported scope is anything but its own one permission (exit 3). The cost is one more registration to make for each purpose, written out in each `outlook_auth.py --help` and README.
- **What `Mail.Read` and `Calendars.ReadBasic` can do, said plainly.** Neither is granted per message, folder or calendar: the token reads every message of the mailbox, and the events of every calendar the account can see (without body, attachments and extensions, for `Calendars.ReadBasic`), and changes none. The narrowing to the inbox and a window is the runtime's. `Mail.ReadBasic` was rejected (no body, no attachments: too little for a snapshot).
- **`providers/_msgraph.py`, moved out of `onedrive.py` and `onedrive_auth.py`.** A `Profile` carries what differs (the permission, the secret, the words of the messages, the test-override names); the functions take the profile. `onedrive.py` keeps every name it had as a thin function over the shared one, and calls its own `secret_resolver`, `keyring_module` and `endpoints` at call time, so the O1 tests that replace them still do. All O1 tests pass; the one edit to them is an import path: the test that reads the callback handler's source for "logs no request line" now reads `providers/_msgraph.py`, where the handler moved. Two behaviours of O1 are stricter, by the lessons of its review applied in one place: a refresh token or client id that is not printable ASCII (the characters `\x21` to `\x7e`) is refused in the record, the rotation and the authorization, and a Graph answer larger than the bound has its own message. `onedrive.py`'s `main` has no last-resort handler (unchanged); `onedrive_auth.py`, now built on the shared `auth_main`, gained one, as did the new mains: an unexpected exception prints its kind and never its text.
- **What the Outlook providers keep (SPEC 3.4).** Fixed API and token hosts; test overrides loopback only and set together, the store then never read (`MAILBOX_OUTLOOK_*`, `INTEGRATION_CALENDAR_OUTLOOK_*`); no redirect followed with a credential; a 60 s timeout; a 429 or 503 reported with its `Retry-After` and never retried; the library errors that quote a header or an address reduced to their kind (`HTTPException` and `ValueError`); the access token validated as printable ASCII before it is sent; the rotation written back only if the stored record still holds the token used; `external_content: true` on every item; ids checked by full-match patterns and percent-encoded before they enter a path. **A next-page address (`@odata.nextLink`) is followed only when it names the request's scheme, host and port (443 written or not) and a path that starts, without case, with `/v1.0/`**, with no user or password, no backslash, no `..` and no character outside `\x21`-`\x7e`; the rest of the path is Graph's to spell (case, `mailFolders('Inbox')`, `/users/<id>/`, a trailing `/`). A link that fails the check is not followed: the pages already read are returned with `truncated: true` and a note on stderr (the first version of the check compared the whole path exactly and made legitimate paging an error; the review found it).
- **Choices where the spec left one (by recommendation; the maintainer may revise).** (1) The mail `search` takes the two fixed boxes of the spec as `--query` (`in:inbox`, `in:inbox is:unread`, the words of the Gmail origins) and refuses any other query with exit 2: Q4. (2) The body is always turned into text here and the service is never asked for a format (no `Prefer` header): the spec found the reference ambiguous, and a conversion by the service would be one whose handling of hidden elements nobody here has read. (3) Attachments are listed by name, declared type and size from the attachment list, never fetched; a list that cannot be read is `null`, not `[]`. (4) Times of the calendar are UTC only; an event Graph gives in another zone is counted in `skipped` and never guessed. (5) `--check` of both reads nothing from Graph, so it does not depend on the calls still to verify. (6) `--own-words` uses `strip_quoted` of `providers/_email_text.py` (moved there by package G1) and prints `own_words_cut` like the Gmail provider; in a checkout without `strip_quoted` the flag is exit 2 with that reason, and a test covers both states. A token, refresh token or id is accepted only as printable ASCII within the bounds of G1's `TOKEN_RE` (4096; 16384 for an access token, which may be a long JWT). (7) The calendar window is at most 62 days (the spec's origins need at most 39). (8) Every `calendarView` request asks `Prefer: outlook.timezone="UTC"`, and an event that still comes in another zone is counted in `skipped` as an anomaly. (9) `--jobs` is at most 4 (Outlook limits concurrent requests on one mailbox; to verify), the attachment list asks no `$top`, and the `requires-python` headers of the four new scripts say `>=3.10`, as the Gmail reader's does, so that `uv` never picks an unpatched system 3.9.6 whose `html.parser` is quadratic on some inputs; their tests still run on 3.9, for compatibility only. A per-message time budget for the HTML-to-text step of the Gmail and Outlook readers is backlog item EM5. (10) A stored record whose `scope` names another permission is not configured (exit 3, before any call), so a record of one provider copied into another's entry is caught. (11) The hidden-text rule of `providers/_email_text.py` (shared with the Gmail reader and the e-mail channel) also removes `mso-hide:all`, `opacity:0` and a zero `width`, `height`, `max-width` or `max-height` together with `overflow:hidden` (all of what is in the element: nothing a child sets undoes them), and text of a zero `font-size` only where it is written: the parser keeps a stack of the sizes, so a descendant with a size of its own (`13px`, `large`) is seen again, as in the MJML-style template that sets `font-size:0` on a wrapper to close the gaps between inline blocks. A first version hid whole template bodies and zero boxes whose text overflows and shows; the review found it. It still reads no style sheet, class, colour or comment, and the provider's help and README say exactly that. The e-mail channel (`email-imap.py`) shares the rule, so its docstring changed too: text hidden by size or opacity is no longer read as text. **The second review of the rule** found three more things, all fixed in the same module: (1) the reverse scan for the element an end tag closes was quadratic (`<span>` x n then `</b>` x n took 27 s at 100 KB, and a 1 MiB body would have taken about forty minutes on the Gmail and Outlook readers, which have no time budget): the open elements are now a stack with an index of the open elements of each name and of the nearest block below each, so an end tag with nothing of its name open returns at once and the total cost is linear (50,000 open tags in under a second), with a depth cap of 4096 past which an element that hides more than its parent hides the rest of the body; (2) mis-nesting let hidden text out (`<span style="font-size:0"><p>a</span>INJECT</p>` read `INJECT`, and so did `display:none`, which predates M1): an end tag with a block open inside the element it names is now ignored, as a browser does, and the same stack serves the hidden elements; (3) visible text was dropped when a browser closes an element by itself (`<p style="font-size:0">x<p>/approve 12`, an `li`, a `td`/`th`, a `tr`, a block after a `p`), and a size set by the `font` shorthand, `calc(...)` or `<font size>` was not read: both modelled now. `visibility:visible` inside a `visibility:hidden` element is left hidden, the safe direction, and said next to the colour limit. **The third review** closed the rest of the cheap gaps: the depth cap now stops reading the body for any element past it (an off-by-one let a hidden wrapper at the edge pass); the skipped elements (script, style, head, title, template, noscript) are a stack and only the innermost one's end tag ends the skip, and textarea, xmp, iframe, noembed, noframes, svg desc and metadata are skipped too, the first five in the parser's raw-text mode; no end tag closes past an open table, td, th, caption, button, object, marquee, applet or select (a table's own end tags close past a cell); `calc()`, `min()`, `max()` and `clamp()` count as zero when every length in them is. The class comment no longer claims more than it models: it lists what is not modelled (the adoption agency algorithm, implied end tags, foster parenting, an empty paragraph from a stray `</p>`, style sheets, classes, colours, position, comments) and says that where it differs from a browser it hides, so some text a browser shows can be dropped and none that a reader cannot see leaks.
- **`"mailbox": "auto"` stops resolving.** A second implementation in `providers/mailbox/` makes the resolution fall through "the only implementation shipped" and exit 3, "none is selected: set MAILBOX_PROVIDER". Nothing in the runtime changed. **The maintainer must set `"mailbox": "gmail"` in the live social handler's `runtime.json` (or `MAILBOX_PROVIDER=gmail` for the tick) before the integration branch reaches `main`**; a value that names an implementation wins over the environment and works today. `providers/mailbox/tests/test_mailbox_resolution.py` pins the exit 3 with the real providers folder, through `providers/resolve.py`, the tick (`scripts/runtime.py`) and the social handler, and the explicit `gmail` working. A fix that keeps `auto` working for existing projects without guessing (for example, `auto` ignoring an implementation that has no record, which is a guess about the project, or a per-project default written by a migration) would change the runtime, so it is proposed in the pull request and not built.
- **The exception to rule 1 of `contracts/secrets.md` now covers three providers** (`onedrive.py`, `outlook.py` of the mailbox and of the calendar), each writing back only its own record, only when it came from the OS secret store and only if it still holds the token the provider used. `contracts/secrets.md` and `providers/secrets/resolver.py` gained two rows (appended after OneDrive's); the OneDrive row's note no longer says it is the one provider that writes its record back.
- **Cost of the safety.** One more Microsoft registration per purpose to make; a mail search that takes two fixed queries; no meeting links, descriptions or attendee lists in the calendar output; an event in a zone other than UTC is left out and counted; a next page that Graph names outside `/v1.0/` or on another host ends a read with the pages already read, marked truncated, and a note. No skill, no agent and no fingerprinted file changed: no bump and no lab run; no call to Microsoft was made (every test runs against a stand-in on loopback).

## 2026-10-18: OneDrive files as sources in the runtime (O2)

The runtime side of `SPEC-ONEDRIVE`: a OneDrive file the person chose in the terminal is a source of type `onedrive`, read through the provider of O1 (`providers/documents/onedrive.py`). The maintainer's answers stand: the delegated `Files.Read` with `offline_access` and nothing else, files only (no folder), the rotated refresh token in the OS secret store (the provider writes it, not the runtime).

- **No picker; the terminal chooses.** Microsoft's picker is a page on its hosts driven by messages, needs SharePoint tokens and narrows nothing under `Files.Read`; the runtime's page and `interface/` stay "nothing from another host". `source-onedrive --search <text>` (or `--folder <path>`) lists up to 25 candidates, numbered, with the `<drive id>/<item id>` to give back, and registers nothing; `source-onedrive --item <drive id>/<item id>` (repeatable) registers and imports each chosen file. The command is terminal only, in the group of no policy, as `source-drive` is: a phone cannot widen what the runtime reads from a person's cloud. File names, paths and search results are another person's text in principle: cleaned to one line, shown, never run or used as a path.
- **What the scope reads, and what the runtime reads.** `Files.Read` lets the token read every file of the person's own OneDrive and writes none; the narrowing to the chosen files is the runtime's (only a registered item id is read, and the listing verbs serve the terminal and register nothing). The docs say so in those words (`sources.md` rule 17, `contracts/runtime.md`, the command's help).
