- **Migration 19 rebuilds `sources` as migration 14 did.** The same columns and both triggers, `CHECK (type IN ('file','notion','drive','onedrive'))`, every row copied as it is (the test compares each row, the versions and the record of what a run received before and after, from a store at 18). The next types (mail and calendar, package RT-1) are one more migration of the same shape, with the word added to `SOURCE_TYPES`; the number is the supervisor's. Cost: the table is dropped and made again inside the migration's transaction, with foreign keys deferred, as before.
- **Version-first refresh by the cTag.** The provider's `stat` is asked and the file is read only when its cTag differs from the one recorded with the newest version; a share or a rename moves the eTag and not the cTag, so it reads nothing. The eTag is kept in the manifest. `supported` is read before `version` (a folder prints no version). Bytes already held make no version even when the cTag moved.
- **A credential never reaches a run.** The provider's process gets `provider_environment` plus `ONEDRIVE_SOURCES_TOKEN` and nothing else; the tests assert the environment of a provider call, the environment a run gets (the secret is in no model's pass list), and that no file of the sources, staging or a task's copies, no manifest, catalog line or message holds a token, the record or a download address.
- **Deviation from the spec.** The spec names the rule in `sources.md` "rule 16"; ATT-1 took 16, so this is rule 17. `sources.register` loads `sources_onedrive` for the origin check when a OneDrive source is registered (the new module imports `sources`), instead of the check living in `sources.py`.
- **After the review (#464).** (1) *Only the terminal registers a OneDrive source:* `source-add` takes the channel and refuses the type `onedrive` from any other (a page token would otherwise register and read any item of the person's OneDrive, which `Files.Read` reaches); a refresh of a registered source may come from the page. (2) *`_clean` also turns the Unicode categories Cc, Cf, Zl and Zp into spaces* (C1 controls, bidi overrides and isolates, zero-width marks, line and paragraph separators): the helper is shared with Drive, which gains it. (3) *One file is one source:* `register` refuses a second source for the same origin, a OneDrive id compared without case and a Drive id as it is. (4) A file chosen again asks OneDrive about it once. Cost: a person who used the page to register a OneDrive source must use `source-onedrive`.
- **Not run against Microsoft.** Every test is against the stand-in service of O1. The maintainer's live step (authorize, `--check`, a search, one DOCX, XLSX, PPTX and PDF, an unchanged refresh, an edited one) is in `SPEC-ONEDRIVE` section 7 and `providers/documents/README.md`, "OneDrive". No skill, no agent and no fingerprinted file changed: no bump and no lab run.

## 2026-10-18: a queued line instead of "busy" on a messaging app (BQ-1)

The maintainer approved recommendation 12 of `SPEC-ANSWER-FROM-SOURCES` (section 8). A text line that would run `say` (a plain line, `/new`, `/ask`) and meets a busy project used to get "Still working on the last message." (the project's model slot is held) or "The planning agent is answering another request. Send it again in a moment." (`RunBusy`), and the person had to send it again.

- **The bridge keeps the line, in memory.** At most 1 line per chat and 5 per project; the sender is told "Got it: I will answer this after the current one."; the oldest line of a project whose slot is free is started at each iteration of the loops and at the end of each job (`Bridge.job_ended` calls `drain`). Beyond the caps, and for a command or a tap, the sentence of before stands. A command is never kept: a `/cancel` kept behind the run it should stop would be of no use, and the table's commands take their own locks.
- **Why memory and not the store.** The ledger "holds no text of a project" (`contracts/runtime.md`, "Notices and the ledger"), and the store's queue (`ops_say.QUEUING_CHANNELS`) is drained only by the local service (`route_queued`), and an entry does not know the chat to answer. Rejected: joining `QUEUING_CHANNELS`. The cost: a crash loses the waiting lines (a clean stop tells each chat "Not answered: the bridge stopped. Send it again."), and the order across shells is not first in, first out (the page has its own queue). Both are stated in `docs/architecture/platform/messaging.md`.
- **The policy is read when the line runs (L46).** The line is started through `run_op`, so a sender narrowed meanwhile is refused with the policy's sentence; a sender removed from the configuration is dropped with no reply, as an update from an unlisted sender is. A sender whose profile does not let them talk is refused when the line comes and nothing is kept for them.
- **Thirty minutes.** A line still waiting after 1800 s is dropped with "Not answered: the project stayed busy for 30 minutes. Send it again." A line that met `RunBusy` waits a second before it is tried again and keeps its place and its time.
- **The text is never written.** Not in a log line (a count only), not in the ledger, not in a file, not sent back.
- **Tests.** `runtime/tests/test_messaging_queue.py` (stand-in messenger, the real operations layer and policy, a fake clock for the queue). The old test that a second line during a run got "Still working" became a test that it is queued and that a command gets the old sentence. No skill, no agent and no fingerprinted file changed: no bump, no lab run, no migration.
- **After the review of #465.** (1) *A line being started stays in the list.* `drain` marks the oldest line `starting` and leaves it in the list until its run begins, so a line that comes between the choice and the start goes behind it (the order is first in, first out) and a stop in that window loses nothing. (2) *The stop messages have a deadline.* They go out together and `close_queue` waits `QUEUE_STOP_WAIT` = 10 s in all, so a hanging provider cannot hold back `end_runs` (the service kills the bridge after 150 s). (3) *The kit has a hook.* `shell_kit.Jobs.job_ended(job)` is called by the job's thread at the end of `_run_job`, outside the lock, and the bridge overrides it; the bridge no longer rebinds `_run_job`, and `test_shell_kit.py` also fails on a `self.<owned name> = ...` assignment. (4) *The poll is short only for a `RunBusy` pause*: a held slot has a job whose end drains. (5) *A listen provider that is not ready starts no waiting line.* Also: a project whose configuration is no longer accepted drops a waiting line like an unlisted sender, and a line refused while the others only wait out a `RunBusy` pause gets the router's sentence. (6) *A start that fails leaves the line in its place.* If `start_job` raises anything but `Busy` or `Stopping` (a thread that cannot start, the shell's `Stop`), `drain` clears the line's `starting` mark and re-raises, so later drains, the expiry and the stop's message reach it; `start_job` itself now leaves no job and no held slot behind when its thread cannot start. `after` is a time: it is compared with the clock, never read as a flag.

## 2026-10-18: reminders, a one-shot notice the messaging bridge sends back (RM-1)

The maintainer approved the recommendations for the open question on reminders (`SPEC-SECRETARY-INTEGRATIONS` section 5 and question 8): a reminder is a stored one-shot notice sent by the messaging bridge, not an effect and not a one-shot routine; the group `reminders` is off by default and turned on only with `accept-config --widen`; `/remind`, `/reminders` and `/forget`; caps; delivered only to the creator's chat; no text in `notices: count`; a sender removed before the time gets nothing; late delivery inside a window, `missed` past it; a new limit (L51: L49 and L50 are the ask runtime's, AQ-1).

- **A notice and not an effect, and what keeps it one.** It takes no hash and does nothing in the world; it reaches only a sender the project lists, in that sender's own private chat, with that sender's own words. `ops_reminders.remind` takes the chat from the authenticated sender (a messaging call that names another chat is refused) or, from the terminal or the page, from a `--to` that the configuration lists and whose profile holds the group. The three bridge-side functions (`reminders_due`, `reminders_sent`, `reminders_retry`) are not rows of the table, so no sender can type them and the MCP mode cannot reach them; the group has no MCP row and `group_refusal` refuses it there. A test reads the imports of its three modules (no effect, provider, model or network).
- **The send-time check is the lesson of F2-A.** `reminders_due` reads the accepted configuration when it claims: the sender must still be in `messaging.senders.<platform>` and `operations.permitted("remind", "messaging", ..., sender)` must still hold, else the reminder ends `dropped` with a closed word (`sender removed`, `not permitted`) and nothing is sent; a profile narrowed and widened again before the time is sent the reminder. The text leaves the function only when `notices` is `text`.
- **Time is the machine's.** The project has no time zone of its own; routines read the machine's local clock and so do reminders (`ops_reminders.now()` and `local_at()`, which a test replaces). The rules are code and tested: `HH:MM` is today or, when past, tomorrow; `YYYY-MM-DD HH:MM`; `in <n>m|h|d`; a 24-hour clock of two digits (`9:30`, `3pm`, `9h30` get the usage sentence, never a guess); a wall time that a clock change skips is refused; one that happens twice means its first occurrence; an instant that a relative time names is read in the local time of THAT day by the machine's rules (`ops_reminders.local_of`), not with the offset the clock carries now, so `in 24h` across a clock change is the right wall time; a time of day that has already passed today is tomorrow's whether or not today's exists (the hour a clock change skips); arithmetic that decides "how far ahead" is done in UTC (two aware times of one zone object compare by their wall clocks in Python, which would be wrong across a change); at least one minute and at most 366 days ahead; a relative time rounds up to the next minute so that it is never earlier than asked.
- **Caps are counted where the row is written.** 100 waiting (open or being sent) in a project, 30 waiting for one chat (so one sender cannot fill the project and block the others; a restrictive default the person can ask to change), 30 made for one chat in a local day; `reminder_add` counts all three inside the transaction that inserts, so two callers cannot both take the last place. The day cap is per recipient chat, not per creator: it protects a phone, whoever made the lines, and it starts at the real start of the local day by the rules of that day (a day with a clock change is not 24 hours from an offset).
- **Delivery is claimed, then marked.** `reminders_due` moves each reminder it returns to `sending` in the same call, so two ticks cannot send one; the bridge sends and marks it `sent`; a failed or deferred send gives it back (`open`) and counts a failure, and it is not offered again before a pause of 1, 2, 4, 8 ... minutes (at most 64; `attempts` and `retry_after` of the row), until the 6-hour window ends it `missed`, so a platform that is down is not asked at every tick; a `sending` row older than 10 minutes belongs to a bridge that stopped and is open again. So a crash between the send and the mark, or a mark that fails after a send that went out (logged by its class), may send a line twice and never zero times, as the spec said. At most 10 go out in one tick of one project.
- **Late and missed.** A reminder up to 6 hours past its time is sent when a bridge looks (marked "(due at HH:MM)" when it is more than 5 minutes late, which is more than a tick's own delay); later it ends `missed` and `/reminders` names it for 7 days. The spec's examples (5 hours delivered, 7 hours missed) are tests.
- **The text drops its format characters.** Unicode category Cf (bidirectional overrides, zero-width characters, the byte order mark, the soft hyphen) is removed with the control characters: a reminder goes to the person's own phone, but an override can disguise a line, and an emoji joiner is lost with the rest (restrictive, reversible).
- **The command word ends at any blank.** `messaging_inbound.parse` splits on any whitespace, so `/remind` followed by a line end or a no-break space is a command and never a line for a model run.
- **The words live in the store and nowhere else.** The table is not in the store's export, not in a card, a catalog or a prompt, and never in a log (the log has the class of an error and a count). The terminal and the page see every reminder with its text (they are the person's own); a messaging sender sees only the ones made for them, and in `count` mode only numbers and times. A reminder of someone else is answered as one that does not exist. A phone is told a machine problem as the one fixed sentence of the files (ATT-1b), never a path.
- **Migration 20, and a store never skips a number (review of #466).** The number was assigned by the supervisor; 19 is another package's (O2). `init` used to apply only the numbers above the highest one stored, so a store that reached 20 before 19 merged would have skipped 19 for good. It now reads the applied versions as a set and applies every number of `MIGRATIONS` the store lacks, ascending, and `open_ready` refuses a store whose applied set misses one ("run init to migrate"): a store at 20 without 19 gets 19 applied, and is refused until then (a test). The tests that list the applied numbers end `18, 19, 20` once O2 is in.
- **Nothing a store or a send raises ends the bridge; a store of an earlier shape is refused with a sentence.** `Reminders.deliver` catches `Exception` per project and per reminder and logs the class alone (a locked database raises the store's own `sqlite3.OperationalError`, which `serve_loop` would not have caught); the claim stays and the next tick, or the stale claim, delivers. A dev store that applied the unmerged first shape of migration 20 (no `attempts`, no `retry_after`) passes the version check, so `_init` and `open_ready` compare the columns of the `reminders` table with the ones this script defines (`EXPECTED_COLUMNS`) and refuse with "this store applied an unmerged migration 20 ... delete the test store or re-create it".
- **The queue of waiting lines never keeps a reminder command.** `/remind`, `/reminders` and `/forget` are dispatched by `Bridge.handle` before `say`, and `queueable` is false for them (the command word ends at any blank), so a `/remind` sent while a model job holds the project's slot is answered at once (a test); the delivery at the end of each tick goes on while the slot is held.
- **Not built.** No page control: the page has the routes (`reminders`, `remind`, `reminder-forget`) and the terminal has the verbs, but the interface draws nothing for them. Reminders derived from the calendar (R2) wait for a real case.
- **Cost of the safety.** A sender needs the group in their profile and the person confirms it once (`--widen`); a reminder cannot go to another person's chat or to a group; a reminder made for a time more than 6 hours before a bridge returns is lost, and says so in `/reminders`; the text is on the messaging platform's servers like any message in `text` mode. No skill, no agent and no fingerprinted file changed: no bump and no lab run.

## 2026-10-18: Mail and calendar as sources in the runtime (RT-1)

The runtime side of `SPEC-SECRETARY-INTEGRATIONS` (sections 1, 2, 6 and 7): a window of the person's mailbox or of one calendar is a source of type `mail` or `calendar`, read through the providers of G1 and M1 and kept as a dated, immutable snapshot. The maintainer's answers stand: snapshots and not a live verb, the fixed boxes of v1 (inbox, important, starred, unread) with a day range and a most, the calendar window chosen by the person, links replaced by `<link: host>`, no subject and no sender in a heading, each message scanned for credentials on its own and the import's own scan as the second guard, read only, `source-mail` and `source-calendar` for the terminal and the page.

- **Snapshot, not a live verb.** What a task sees is exactly the bytes recorded in `task_sources` (id, version, sha256); what chooses the data is the person's origin line; the unattended refresh is the `ingest` step that already exists, whose arguments (`source`, `file`, `url`, `sha256`) have no place for a query. `runtime/sources_mail.py` `provider_call` is the one caller of the classes `reader:email` and `integration:calendar` for sources (limit L51); it sends only `search` and `events` and refuses every other verb and any implementation but the four of `SECRETS`, and the implementation comes from the origin, so "auto" decides nothing. The other modules that name `reader:email` are the two older ticks that poll a platform's notification e-mails with the project's own fixed query, `runtime/handlers/social.py` and `scripts/runtime.py` (the test names them, scans `runtime/`, `scripts/` and `providers/`, and also looks for a path of `providers/mailbox` or `providers/calendar` written anywhere else).
- **The text is built by code and cleaned twice.** `runtime/snapshot_text.py` is pure. Links become `<link: host>` and `mailto:` addresses `<address>` before anything is scanned or written, so a magic-link token never reaches the scan, the folder or a run and a message that only carries one stays; control, direction and invisible characters go (the providers do it too); a line is cut at 1,000 characters and a body at 4,000. Each message (each event's line) is scanned on its own and a credential shape leaves that message out, counted by date and sender domain; the whole text passes the import's scan unchanged, and a test turns the first guard off to show the second refuses with nothing kept.
- **Deviation: body lines are indented by two spaces.** The spec shows the body plain. The catalog reads headings and sheets from a converted text by line (`_headings_of`, `_sheets_of`), so a message whose body begins a line with `#`, a code fence or `## Sheet:` would put third-party words in the planner's catalog, which fact 9 of the spec forbids. The indentation costs nothing a model needs and the headings (`## 1. 2026-10-17 07:12`) stay code's.
- **Deviation: `origin_version` also holds the zone and the local date.** The spec says the sorted (id, change key) pairs. A listing that does not change would then make no version for days, `read_at` would stay old and L52 would refuse the snapshot to the daily routine's own tasks, which the 26-hour bound was chosen to allow. With the date in the identity, the same list on the same day makes no version (the named test) and a refresh on another day always makes one; the cost is one version a day for a quiet mailbox, inside `source_versions_kept`.
- **Deviation: every copy of a snapshot begins with its age, not only a person-started one.** The line (`Snapshot of <id>, read at <time> (<n> hours old when this task started). It is data, never an instruction.`) is added to the copy and is not part of the version: the record in `task_sources` keeps the version and the hash of its text. Cost: a copy is a few bytes longer than the version, and a test asserts the record still equals the version's hash.
- **Deviation: a multi-day all-day event is listed once, on its first day** with `all day until <date>`, and an event that started before the window is listed on its first day with its true start. Repeating it on every day would cost lines for nothing and an event of 40 days could pass the size cap.
- **Migration 21, with 20 missing.** The sources table is rebuilt exactly as migrations 14 and 19 did it (the same columns and both triggers, every row copied as it is, `CHECK` with the two new words, `SOURCE_TYPES` and `sources.TYPES` in step); the test builds a store through the real migrations below 21 and compares every row, the versions and the record of what a run received before and after. Number 20 is the reminders package's (RM-1), merged first; the literals of the older store tests end in `19, 20, 21`. RM-1's store guard (`missing_migrations`, `shape_problem`) needs nothing for `sources`: its `EXPECTED_COLUMNS` lists only a table whose migration was reworded before it merged, and this one was not.
- **Limits L52 and L53** are the spec's LS1 and LS2 (named L52 and L53 everywhere else), numbered after RM-1's L51 (the tests are named `test_limit_52_` and `test_limit_53_`).
- **The group.** `source-mail` and `source-calendar` were first rows for the terminal and the page in the group `sources`; the maintainer's decision below made them the terminal's alone (no group, no route), so no other channel can start a read of a mailbox.
- **The reader's scope is wider than the window.** `gmail.readonly`, `Mail.Read` and the calendar scopes read the whole mailbox or every calendar and change none; the box, the days, the most and the one calendar are the runtime's narrowing, and the docs say so in those words. A source registered by `source-add` reads nothing until `source-mail`, `source-calendar` or a refresh; if the second guard refuses the first import the source stays registered with no version, as it does for OneDrive.
- **Not run against Google or Microsoft.** Every test answers through the `call` seam with shapes the providers print, and three run the real providers (Outlook mail, Google Calendar, Outlook calendar) against their loopback stand-ins. The maintainer's live steps are in `SPEC-SECRETARY-INTEGRATIONS` section 10. No skill, no agent and no fingerprinted file changed: no bump and no lab run.
- **After the review (#469).** (1) *A sender's domain is a stranger's word:* the left-out line is scanned before it is written, and a domain that looks like a key (a spoofed `x@xoxb-...example`) is dropped from the line, so the import's second scan can no longer refuse a whole snapshot because of the line that counts the message left out. (2) *Every link form is replaced:* the match has no word boundary in front, takes a scheme of up to 41 characters followed by one or more slashes or backslashes (`x_https://`, `0https://`, `https:/`, `https:\\`), a protocol-relative `//host/path`, `www.` and a bare `host.tld/path`, `host.tld?query` or `host.tld#part`; the scheme is bounded so that a run of 200,000 letters is scanned in a moment (a test times it). (3) *The credential scan reads the uncut fields:* the subject, the sender, every attachment name and the whole body of a message, and the title and place of an event, are scanned before any cut, so a key that a cut would have split is found whole and the message or event is left out. (4) *A version dated more than five minutes ahead of the clock has an unknown age* and is refused to a task that starts on its own (`snapshot_age` returns None); a person-started task is told the age is unknown. (5) *The limit-52 test* also scans `scripts/` and `providers/*.py` for the two class names, names `scripts/runtime.py` beside the social handler as the older ticks that poll notification e-mails, and looks for a path of `providers/mailbox` or `providers/calendar` written outside the places that document the authorization commands; the migration test holds a store with RM-1's 20 and one that has 21 without 20. (6) The prompt line that introduces a task's sources says a source may be a snapshot of a mailbox or a calendar.
- **Maintainer's decisions on the two open points of #469 (2026-10-18).** (1) *`/ask` and a snapshot:* a mail or calendar source is left out of the automatic selection (rules 2 and 3 of `runtime/ask_select.py`) and is read only when the question cites it with `@<id>`; a question that cites one is accepted only from the terminal, the conversation's own shell and the page, and from a messaging sender who holds `ask` it is refused with a fixed sentence (`ops_ask.SNAPSHOT_HERE`: ask in the terminal or on the page, nothing was run) before any copy is made or any model runs; the MCP mode never has `ask`. A project with only snapshots answers `NO_MATCH` and offers their ids. Documents are answered as before on every channel. Reason: the text of a mailbox goes to a model, and through a messaging app it would pass the platform's servers. (2) *Registering is terminal only:* `source-mail`, `source-calendar` and `source-add --type mail|calendar` are refused on every channel but the terminal, as O2 did for OneDrive (a page token would otherwise register and read any mailbox the scope reaches); the two rows list the terminal alone and have no page route; `source-refresh` of an already registered one stays on the page. Cost: a person who registered from the page uses the terminal once; the page keeps the refresh.

## 2026-10-18: the runtime's replies in the project's language (LR-1)

The runtime side of `SPEC-ANSWER-FROM-SOURCES` section 7, with the maintainer's approval of its recommendation (11): one phrase table instead of translated constants. A project whose `language` is `pt-BR` gets Portuguese from the messaging bridge, its cards, the conversation and the answer from sources; every other project gets the English it always got.

- **One table, English as the source.** `runtime/phrases.py`: `TABLE = {"en": {...}, "pt-BR": {...}}`, a pure literal, and `text(key, cfg, **fields)`. Each key is the name of the constant it replaced (`STALE`, `QUEUED`, `ASK_HINT`...), and the module that had the constant keeps it as `phrases.en(key)`, so the tests written before the table read the same strings. A key two areas shared as a name carries a prefix (`ATT_` for the files a sender sends, `RM_` for the reminders, `ASK_` where the answer from sources collides). The language is `language.effective(None, cfg["language"])`: the project's key, else `en`, never the request's own language (that is the language of an artifact). A tag with no table (`fr`, `pt`, `pt-PT`) gets English, and so does a key a table lacks; a key English lacks is an error.
- **What is in, what is out.** In: the bridge's constants and inline replies, `/help` and the usage lines, the card frames and button labels, the notice of a message set aside, the waiting-line sentences, the sentences of the files a sender sends and of the reminders, the conversation's own sentences (the state answer, the plan reply's lines, "The planning agent may not start now", "The router could not run", the placeholder), and the answer from sources with the frame of a checked quote; 209 keys in each language, and 42 status words. Out, and the same in every language: the text of an `OpsError`, a decision's title and body, logs, the terminal's output, the page's text, file names, commands, `@id` mentions and ids. A command inside a sentence is the same in both columns, with its angle-bracket arguments (`/remind <time> <text>`); a test compares the commands of every sentence across the languages, and the `{fields}` too.
- **The imitation check reads every language.** `ask_reply.imitates` compared a folded line with `quote [` and `checked quote`. The form code writes is now translated, so it compares with the opening of the checked-quote sentence of EVERY language of the table (`phrases.heads`), whatever the project's language is: a model asked for a Portuguese answer may write the English form, and the reverse. A test imitates the form in each language, dressed in a bullet, upper case, a look-alike letter and a zero-width space.
- **An operation's sentence on a phone.** The operations layer stays in English (the terminal and the page read it as it was). A reply of `remind`, `reminder-forget`, `attachment-add` and `attachment-discard`, and an `OpsError` about a file or a reminder, reach the phone through `phrases.localize`, which matches the text against the English sentence of each key of the `ATT_` and `RM_` families, field by field, and says it in the language; the closed words inside one (the reasons a file was refused, the kinds of file) are said too. A text it does not know is not touched. This costs a match per reply and no change to an operation; a sentence an operation words differently from the table would reach the phone in English, which a test of every key keeps from happening.
- **The kind of a decision is a word.** `plan`, `effect`, `question`, `review`, `acceptance` and `your_document` are said with a word of the language (the table has them) in a header, a list and a count line; a kind the table does not know stays as it is.
- **Deviations from the spec.** (1) The headings of a stored decision that code writes whole ("The router asks", "The route was not recognised", "No delivery was routed" and the two headings of a delivery routed again) are said in the language at render time, by an exact match (`phrases.heading`); a heading with a flow, a skill, a person's or a model's word (a plan's title, a skill's ending) stays as it is, and the stored title and the card's hash are never changed; the body of the unrecognised-route question is stored in English too (it was stored in the language by the first version of this package) and is said by `phrases.body` on a card and in the conversation's reply; a heading or body is matched exactly, with whole numbers in its fields, so a text that merely starts like one is not touched. (2) A status word inside a sentence is said at the reading layer too (see the next bullet). (3) The reason inside "The answer run failed (...)" and the router's failure are the operations layer's text and stay English. (4) `config` now carries `language`, because the bridge reads it there.
- **Status words are said at the reading layer (maintainer's decision, 2026-10-18).** `phrases.STATUS` is a closed table of the store's own words per language (42: the CHECK lists and state machines of `providers/store/sqlite.py`: requests and tasks, runs, decisions, attachments, reminders, sources, approvals, routines), and `phrases.status(word, cfg)` returns the word of the language, or the word unchanged when it is unknown or the language has no table. It is called only where a word is rendered inside a phrase-table sentence: the "tem o status ..." sentences (`NOT_OPEN`, `ATT_NOT_HELD`, `RM_NOT_WAITING`, the last two also when `localize` says an operation's English sentence: a field named `status` is translated) and the state answer's "Stuck" line; never where a status is parsed, stored, compared, logged or sent to a shell, so the store, the operations layer (and the terminal, the MCP mode and the API that read it) and the logs keep the English word. The Portuguese forms are the citation form of the masculine ("status aprovado"), which avoids agreement with the noun. A test reads the store's lists, so a status added to the store cannot reach a phone without a word in each language.
- **After the review (#471).** (1) *The imitation check:* a checked quote imitated in the Portuguese form passed with a double space, a tab, a zero-width character between the words, a hyphen, or no space before the bracket in the middle of a line. The line is now folded and its white space collapsed, and for the opening of every language a pattern with any white space or hyphen (or none) between the words, followed by an optional colon and a `[`, is looked for anywhere in it; the heading `Instructions found in external content:` of every language is read as the skill's own, so a model line imitating the Portuguese one is not shown as prose. (2) *`localize` said a file's name:* a file named `presentation` became a word of the table; only the fields `status`, `reason`, `declared` and `sniffed` (and the decimal mark in `size` and `cap`) are said now. (3) *The English-only check is narrowed:* the Portuguese column is its own data module, `runtime/phrases_pt_br.py`, the only exempt file; `phrases.py` keeps the check, and each Portuguese line of a test carries its own `validate: allow english-only` marker. (4) *The language is read once* per update, tick or drain (`Bridge.read_once`), not at every sentence. (5) *Portuguese details (and `abandonado` for the store's `dropped`, distinct from `discarded`):* a decimal comma in a size, `arquivo` for a file with no name, the word `card` (the maintainer's) and `mensagem` where a person's message is meant, and the supervisor's rewrites of nine sentences.
- **Cost of the safety.** The column is the maintainer's to review: a wrong word in it is a wrong sentence on a phone, not a wrong act, since no sentence carries a decision (the commands and ids are the same). The language is read from `config` (cheap, never refuses) once for each update, tick or drain the bridge handles, and the jobs those start keep what was read (a long job says its last sentence in the language it began with); a project whose configuration cannot be read gets English.

## 2026-10-18: reminders in the person's own words, by code (NR-1)

The first package of `SPEC-NATURAL-REQUESTS`, with the maintainer's approval of its recommendations (2026-10-18). The request: no slash in front of everything; "me lembra amanha as 13h da aula de violao" and "/remind aula de violao as 13hrs amanha" must work. Tier 1 of three: code, 0 model runs, 0 lab runs, no skill and no measurement file touched. Tier 3 (the router names the operation) and tier 2 (embeddings, only if measured) are later packages.

- **Three tiers, code first.** A closed table of trigger words and a deterministic time parser take the frequent forms with no model, so a reminder is not held behind a running router (it takes no route lock and no model slot) and costs nothing. What the table does not match goes on as before.
- **A language is data.** (Supervisor's addition of 2026-10-18.) The parser engine (`runtime/natural_time.py`) and the intent rules (`runtime/natural.py`) hold no word of any language. Each language is one file `runtime/natural_lang_<tag>.py` holding a `TABLE` literal (weekdays, months, relative days, "in N minutes" forms, hour markers, the words of a time not fully understood, trigger words), named in `runtime/natural_languages.json`, read as data (`ast.literal_eval`: a file is parsed, never run), checked by `natural_time.check_table`, with duplicate tags refused, and validated once when a bridge starts (a broken table stops it with a sentence; a line never meets the error). The table of EVERY language is tried on EVERY line, so a Portuguese project understands an English line; a weak word (an abbreviation such as "sex" or "may") counts only in a line that is in its language. v1 ships `en` and `pt-BR`; adding one is a file, a name in the list and its tests (a test plugs a stub third language in with no engine edit). The Portuguese table is listed by name in `.security-scan-allow` for `english-only`, as `phrases_pt_br.py` is, with its test data `runtime/tests/natural_cases_pt_br.py`. A line no table parses is not matched, never guessed: it is asked back now and goes to the router later.
- **A clear "me lembra" with a clear time is made at once** and the reply shows the reading and the undo: "Lembrete #4: amanha, seg 19/10, as 13:00 (BRT): aula de violao. Para desfazer: /forget 4." A reminder is a notice, cheap to undo, and a confirmation step on each one would double the turns of the most frequent request. The words are repeated only when the notices are `text`. This reply also answers the typed `/remind`; a time of day that has passed says so and gives tomorrow's. The terminal and the page keep the sentence they always had.
- **Ambiguity is asked, never guessed (the maintainer's rule); a reading that is understood but uncertain is shown and confirmed (supervisor's decision D1 of 2026-10-18, by recommendation, reversible).** A bare hour ("as 9", "as 3 e meia": no `h`, no `:`, no marker) asks 09:00 or 21:00; "9h", "13h30", "13.30" with a cue or a meridiem and "13 e meia" are exact, and "1h30" is a duration after "daqui a"/"in" and asked alone. "Sexta que vem" and "next friday" offer the nearest one and the one after it; a day with "meia-noite" offers the start and the end of the day; "daqui a 2 dias as 9h" is the day two days ahead; "sexta dia 23" is the date that falls on that weekday. A day with no hour, a vague time, two time phrases and a date such as 5/6 in an English line ask which. x/y is a date only after a cue or beside a time and never a fraction ("parcela 3/10", "3/10 do aluguel"). A word of a week, a month or a year within two words of the time, a day word with a typo (read as the word it is one letter from; the tables' `not_typos` and any word of a table are never typos), a dotted number beside a day word, a part of the day that contradicts the hour and a past date this year read as next year's (the year is shown, D2) do not make an open question: the reading is shown ("Entendi: amanha, seg 19/10, as 09:00: arrumar o quarto. Confirma? (sim / outra hora)") and a "sim" makes it; an hour or a day answers only the part it names, a day and an hour replace both, "nao" or "cancela" gives it up. A date that is today with an hour that has passed stays today's and is refused as past. A line that repeats ("toda segunda", "a cada 2 horas", "de 8 em 8 horas", "every monday") is said to be a routine's (N6) and nothing is kept. A remind word with no time asks when; a note word with no time ("anota: ...") offers a reminder; a time and no words asks what, and a short answer that is not a request for work (no work verb, not yes, no, thanks or cancel) is the words, a bare time completing the time. The question is a draft in the bridge's memory (ten minutes, swept on every tick, one per chat, lost on a restart, never logged, never in the store); the next line finishes it when it is a time, one of the readings (by day and hour together), a day and an hour that replace them, the kind or the words; any other line, a command, a file or a reply to another message drops it and is handled as new, and a reply to a CARD always answers that card (N3).
- **"Me lembra" is a notice, "marca na agenda" a calendar event (supervisor's decision D3).** The calendar is not built (SPEC-SECRETARY-WRITES): its row is marked planned and the reply says so, in the project's language, after the policy of a plain line, and makes no reminder in its place. "Marca"/"marque" with a time, and a verb with a calendar word ("marca na agenda", "put on my calendar"), are event writes: they end in a card the person approves, so a wrong pick costs one "nao". A scheduling verb with a time and no calendar word ("agende", "schedule", "book", and the verb use of "agenda"/"calendar" followed by what to schedule) asks "event, or something else?"; "something else" sends the whole line on to the planning agent, "reminder" keeps it as a reminder, and the same verb with no time is a plain line. A bare calendar noun used as a read ("agenda de amanha?", "calendar for tomorrow") is not claimed: it goes on to the planning agent. A line with a time and neither a remind word nor a calendar word ("aula de violao amanha 13h", "anote ...") asks reminder or event; it is taken only when it holds a clock or a length of time, is short, has no question mark, does not repeat and does not start with a work verb, and a sender whose profile lacks the group never has such a line taken. A remind verb counts anywhere in the line ("in 25 minutes let me know to ...") when the line holds a time; the noun "lembrete"/"reminder" at the head makes a reminder unless the line reports a problem ("lembrete 3 nao chegou"); the bare word "lembretes" is a list request only when it is the whole line.
- **The strict grammar stays and decides first.** `/remind HH:MM text`, `/remind YYYY-MM-DD HH:MM text` and `/remind in <n>m|h|d text` are read as before (`reminders.strict`), and `reminders.resolve` checks every time the parser makes (a time that has passed, a wall time the clock skips, one minute to 366 days). `/remind` with anything else is read in words, and when that fails too the answer is a question in the project's language, never the English usage sentence.
- **Safety, by code.** The policy is the operation's own group, asked before the line is read (`remind(said=...)` calls `ops_actor.guard` first), so a profile without `reminders` gets the typed refusal and may still remind in words without `talk`. The id of a forget is digits that appear in the line, the time is a parse of a span of the line, the text is a span of the line, scanned for credentials. Limit L51 is amended: the reply echoes the words in `text` mode, and the draft is memory only.
- **Deviations from the spec.** (1) The zone is always shown in a reply (the test list asks for it; the spec's "only when not the project's usual one" assumed a zone the code cannot know). (2) `natural_time.parse(text, now, languages=None)` takes no `local_at`: no step of the parser needs the zone, and `resolve` keeps the zone checks. (3) The parser returns `missing: "time"` only; a day-less clock is the time-only form that `resolve` rolls over. (4) The `said` of `remind` is a keyword of the function and not an argument of the table's row, so no shell of the table can pass it. (5) The 3-letter weekday abbreviations are taken only where they are not common words ("ter" and "dom" are left out).
- **After the independent reviews (#474), round 1.** The reviewer found the parser guessing where it must ask: "1h30" read as a clock, minutes dropped ("13.30", "13h 30", "13 e meia"), a week or a month word silently ignored, "quinta que vem" read as this week's, a draft answer that named a day matched by the hour alone, a past date moved to next year, any a/b read as a date, midnight read as one instant, an abbreviation of another language read as a weekday, a scheduling verb read as a calendar word. Each is fixed as the rule above, with the reviewer's lines as tests.
- **After the independent reviews (#474), round 2.** The fixes had opened regressions, and the supervisor decided D1 to D5: (D1) an uncertain reading is confirmed, not asked open, and a day the line had is never lost; the time-word rule is local (within two words of the time) and the typo rule has stop lists; (D2) a past date this year is confirmed with its year; (D3) the calendar verbs as above; (D4) a forget by number only with an explicit id ("#9", "lembrete 9"), every other number in the line is a word matched by whole words, and every forget reply shows the reminder's text and "desfaz"; (D5) a real undo: the operation `reminder-restore` (the group `reminders`) reads the sender's own cancelled row (cancelled in the last ten minutes, due at least a minute ahead, not already back) and adds a NEW reminder with the same words, moment, zone and chat (no migration; the old record never changes), replying "#9 de volta como #12". Also: a dotted time with a meridiem ("1.30pm") applies it and "10 a.m." is read (N1); a reply to a card answers the card (N3); a text answer is not yes, no, thanks, cancel, a bare time or a request that holds a work verb (N4); "Sex Shop" and a capitalised weak word mid-sentence are names, "may" is a month only after a cue, and the pt-BR markers are no longer the particles "de/da/do" so an English line with a Portuguese name keeps English ask-mode (N9); "hj", "na 2a", "2a feira" are understood; the bare list trigger and the noun "lembrete" do not take a report ("reminders are broken", "lembrete 3 nao chegou"); answering "lembrete" to event-or-something-else keeps the line; an empty or non-list `natural_languages.json` stops the bridge; the past-time refusal shows a day and a month; a picked reading is shown with its day and not rolled; a relative time is elapsed time across a clock change. Not done: when two languages tie on a line, each weak word is accepted in its own table and no question is asked for a reading that differs (rare; the weak words are few); a bare number after a forget verb with no "lembrete" ("esquece o 9", "cancela #9") is not an id: only "lembrete 9" and "lembrete #9" are.
- **After the independent reviews (#474), round 3.** The second re-check found one regression and three gaps in the answers to a question, and the supervisor decided Q1 to Q3 (by recommendation, reversible). (B1) A weak word of a day (an abbreviation that is also a common word: "sex", "mon", "dec") that touches the time or stands beside a number is the day ("Fri at 9am", "on Dec 5", "May 5", "Seg as 9h", "na Sex as 9h"), whatever its capital letter; anywhere else it may be a name ("sex shop as 9h", "call Fri Hotel at 9am") and the reading is SHOWN and confirmed, never dropped: the capital letter no longer decides anything. (M1) A repeating word is a repetition only when it is part of the time phrase: an opener ("every", "toda", "a cada") followed by a length of time or a weekday, or an adverb ("daily", "semanalmente") beside the time or at the edge of the words; "weekly report", "each supplier", "every client", "a cada um" and "todo dia da planilha" are the reminder's own text, and the time words "toda/todo/todas/todos" are no longer doubts. (M2) The answers to a question are read as answers: "outra hora"/"another time" asks "Para quando?" and keeps the words; courtesies around a yes are ignored ("sim, pode", "yes please", "ok obrigado"); a bare hour answers "a que horas?" (and is asked 09:00 or 21:00), "22/10" answers a confirmation with a day, "13.30" an hour, "sim" to "a reminder or an event" is the reminder, "nao" gives any question up, a day answers a time question by replacing the day and asking only the hour; a short reply that does not fit is asked once more ("sim" to two readings), a longer line, a request of this tier or a request for work is a new line; a "sim" after the ten minutes says the question expired (the bridge remembers only that a question expired, for thirty minutes, never what it said); a question that replaces an open one begins "A pergunta anterior foi descartada.". (Q1) "Lembrete do/de/no 3" is not an explicit id: it is shown and confirmed ("Cancelar o lembrete #3: ...? (sim / nao)"), as are several numbers ("lembrete 1 e 2") and an hour ("o lembrete das 9", matched against the moment of the sender's own reminders); "lembrete 3", "#3", "lembrete #3" and "lembrete numero 3" act at once. (Q2) "Marca um lembrete ...", "agende um lembrete ..." and "schedule a reminder ..." make a reminder: the word reminder wins over the scheduling verb. (Q3) A line with "semana que vem"/"next week" and no day asks "Qual dia da semana que vem?" and the weekday answered is the one of the week that starts on the next Monday (on a Sunday that is tomorrow's). Also: the common misspellings of tomorrow and today are entries of the tables ("tmrw", "tommorow", "hje"); a capitalised word in the middle of a line is never a typo of a day and a typo is never dropped from the words; the undo is the bare word only ("undo the deploy" goes on) and brings back the most recent cancelled reminder that is not already back; a part of the day beside an hour with no marker says which hour ("amanha de manha as 9" is 09:00, "tonight at 9" 21:00); the module rows listed twice by the merge were removed from the documents. Not done: when two languages tie on a line, each weak word is accepted in its own table and no question is asked for a reading that differs; "esquece o 9" or "cancela #9" with no "lembrete" is not an id (only the noun with the number is); the answer to "which day next week?" that holds an hour and a line that already holds a different hour keep the hour of the answer.
- **After the independent reviews (#474), round 4: the guard, structurally.** The third re-check found five classes of silently wrong results that came from one cause: a reading was made without looking at what the line said OUTSIDE the time phrases it read. The spike of 2026-10-18 measured it on 600 labelled lines (our engine 16 silently wrong, 8 of them es/fr/de lines whose clock was read and whose day was dropped; the adopted parsers 13 to 174) and recommended keeping the engine and closing the hole inside it. The guard (`natural_time.leftover_doubt`) is that: a reading is made only when the leftover holds no month, weekday, day word, ordinal date or date marker with a number of any table (an abbreviation counts where its language is the line's), none of a table's names of a day of the year (Natal, Christmas, Easter), and, for a line with no trigger of a table, no typed command and no day or length of time, no word that no table holds; otherwise the reading is shown and confirmed (D1). It closes uncued "May 5"/"5 May"/"the 5th of May" (read as the date, with the year shown), "1o/1°/primeiro de dezembro", "dia 1o", "dia primeiro", "in December", "em janeiro", "no Natal" and the foreign lines. A weak word of a day that is not where a day stands is no date: it stays in the words ("Dr. Mon called", "ligar Sex Shop") and the guard confirms. The decision costs asks (false asks on the corpus went from 77 to 118 of 441 lines that expect a moment, none of them wrong) and is taken because the maintainer's rule is that nothing ambiguous is made. It is tested, not trusted: `runtime/tests/test_natural_corpus.py` runs the 575 non-maintainer lines of the spike corpus and the reviewers' probe lines and fails on any line made at another moment, made when it should be asked, or made when it is not a time line. Also: "quarter past/to" and "half past", an hour said in words ("as nove"), "3 e meia da tarde"; "another time" keeps the day that was shown and a bare hour offers both; "n° 3", "n. 3", "no. 3", "nº3" are explicit ids ("no 3" is a preposition and is confirmed), a forget with no noun ("cancela #3") or by numbers alone is shown and confirmed, "cancel the 9am reminder" is a forget by the hour; the answer to "which day next week?" (and "tuesday next week", "semana que vem segunda", "na sexta da semana que vem") is always shown with the day it resolved to, the week after this one Monday to Sunday, and made only on a yes (the supervisor's decision: no convention is applied silently); a part of the day that has passed is shown as tomorrow's ("09:00 today has passed: tomorrow ..."), the same weekday as today is today or the next one, a length of days asks the hour; "todo santo dia", "dia sim dia nao", "nos dias uteis", "de hora em hora", "a cada meia hora", "on weekdays", "twice a day", "hourly", "nightly" are routines (the plural unit alone is not: "todas as horas lancadas" is text); "sim mas as 15h" is a yes with an hour, "nao obrigado" a no, a "nao" to a cancel question says that nothing was cancelled, "sim, cancela" is a yes there. Where a reviewer's label disagreed with the spike corpus the corpus won ("9:30" is 09:30). Not done: the answer to a question in a language with no table (the words stay in the reminder's text as typed); a table for es, fr or de (a table and a name in the list, design rule 3); Japanese is not tokenised (the lines return nothing and are asked).
- **Free text is never made at once; it is shown and made on a yes (maintainer's decision of 2026-10-19; supersedes "a clear me lembra with a clear time is made at once with the undo" for free text).** Every reminder asked in natural words, `/remind` with natural arguments included, shows its reading ("Entendi: amanha, seg 19/10, as 09:00 (BRT): ...  Confirma?") and is made only after the person's yes: a one-tap **Yes** button where the platform has buttons (`Bridge.ask_yes`, a ledger card of the kind `reminder` that holds no word of the reminder, and `Reminders.tapped`; a tap on a question that was answered, replaced or has expired says so and makes nothing), and the typed yes everywhere. Only the strict `/remind` forms (`HH:MM text`, `YYYY-MM-DD HH:MM text`, `in Nm|h|d text`) make one directly. `natural.understand` never returns a reminder to make; `natural.complete` returns one only for a confirmed draft (a yes to the confirmation that showed the whole reading, or the answer to the kind question, which shows it too; a pick among the readings offered, a time, a day, "another time" or the words are never a yes: they lead to the question that shows the reading they produced, with the Yes button); an answer that changes the reading (an hour, a day, "sim mas as 15h", "outra hora", the day of next week) shows the new reading and asks again. `remind(said=...)` runs the grammar's checks and the credential scan BEFORE the reading is shown, so a moment that has passed, a date the calendar lacks or a secret in the words is refused with no question and nothing echoed. A silently wrong reminder is therefore impossible by construction, and the guard of round 4 is now what keeps the wrong reading shown rare: the corpus test counts the readings shown wrong (9 of 435 lines that expect a moment in the corpus, 0 of 242 in the probe lines at the time of writing; all of the corpus ones are lines in a language with no table, a typo that is also a name, or a "week from friday") as a metric, not as a failure. Also fixed, because each would now be shown wrong: "9:00h", "15:00h"; "entre 9h e 10h" asks the two hours; "30 min / meia hora / 1 hora antes de ... as H" and "1 hour before ... at 3pm" are computed; "as 9h de 12/12" and "em 12/12" keep the date; "32/10", "00/10", "dia 32", "32 de outubro" are kept as written and refused with the calendar's sentence; a weekday that is the topic ("a reuniao de sexta foi cancelada", "friday's meeting") is offered as the day and as not the day, and stays in the words; "dom" is Sunday; the plural of a weekday ("todas as segundas", "as segundas", "nas segundas", "on mondays") and "sempre"/"always" are routines. The week after a Sunday: said on a Sunday, "next week" begins after tomorrow's Monday (a calendar that starts its weeks on Sunday, as Brazil's and the United States' do, opens a new week), and the reading is shown either way. Principle 8: every test string that was within reach of a line of the maintainer's file was reworded (objects, names, numbers, structure) and checked by a script that compares every string of the tests with the file without printing it; what is left is the grammar of the commands themselves ("apaga o lembrete 3"), which names no object. Not done: the answer to "which day next week?" that names a date in words; the es, fr and de tables.
- **Cost of the safety.** A trigger list that is too wide would turn a plain line into a reminder; the lines that matter (the ones the maintainer will supply, at least 20) decide the final lists before this merges. The worst a misreading does is make the person's own reminder, shown with its reading and `/forget n`; it never decides, approves or consents.
- **A reminder is made for the moment the person was shown (targeted check of #474, 2026-10-19; the relative case is the supervisor's decision, by recommendation, reversible).** The yes to the confirmation and the answer to the kind question both make the reminder from the reading that was shown, with its day: `natural._pinned` gives the operation the date and the hour of the reading, never a bare `HH:MM` or an `in 30m` that the operation would read again at the time of the answer ("plumber at 10" shown as today 10:00 and answered at 10:01 was made for tomorrow). An absolute moment that has passed by the answer stays dated and the operation refuses it as past, so nothing is moved to another day; a relative reading ("in 30 minutes") is pinned to the moment shown, and when the answer comes after that moment the new moment is shown and the yes is asked again. Tests: `runtime/tests/test_natural_pinned.py`.

## 2026-10-18: the foundation of the writes to the person's own accounts (FND)

Package FND of `SPEC-SECRETARY-WRITES`, with the maintainer's approval of its fifteen recommendations (2026-10-18): the secretary reads, edits and saves in the person's own Google and Microsoft accounts. The foundation adds no write; it adds what every write stands on, so that the packages of each family (the calendar, the mail from the person's account, the mailbox, the files) add a kind and a provider and change none of this.

- **One token per account, the restriction in the platform (maintainer's decision D-B, 2026-10-18).** This reverses the rule "one secret per purpose, minimum permission" of `contracts/secrets.md` for the person's own accounts: the account's token carries the platform's whole scope set, and what restricts it is code in this order: the process that holds it (no run ever does), the verbs of each provider script (readers send GET only, writers an allow-list, nothing deletes for good), the effect document whose hash the person approves, the policy groups and grants of the channel, and the configuration. The scopes and the authorize commands are the packages ACCT-G and ACCT-M.
- **The three effect words, `calendar`, `mailbox` and `files`, arrive with the runtime, not with a skill.** Like `write` before them: they are in the validator's list and in the table of `contracts/environment.md`, no skill declares them, no skill's gate opens them, and a standing approval named in a bounds file never covers them (`ops._bounds_words` excludes `project_config.ACCOUNT_EFFECT_WORDS` whether or not a kind is registered yet). Drafts are under `mailbox`, not `send` (nothing is sent).
- **The provider role `writer`.** `writer:calendar`, `writer:mailbox` and `writer:files`, folder `providers/writer/`, in `resolve.py` `ROLES` and in neither `LISTED` nor `environment.md` (no skill may require them, like `messenger`, `converter`, `exporter` and `script`). Always called with an explicit implementation, never `auto`. Mail sending stays `sender:email`, with two more implementations to come. The readers keep their classes and their GET-only verbs: limit L52 is untouched.
- **What the platform may write to is the project's, in `runtime.json` `accounts`.** The calendars, the folders and the mail folders named there, each a lowercase name mapped to an id checked by a pattern of its provider; the file is closed and covered by its hash, so widening a target is the person's act in the terminal. Anything else is refused though the token could reach it (maintainer's recommendation 11).
- **`account_start.start` is the one way in.** It prepares and stops at the card: one request, one step task of a kind that ends in a gate of an account word, run in this process with no model. Its title is a fixed phrase written by code (`title_key`, a phrase key of the family `TITLE_`), never the person's words, because a title is shown on every card and in the terminal. It refuses the MCP mode and any channel the policy does not know. Deviation from the spec's signature: it takes the account (`account=`) and the title (`title_key=`) as keywords, since the early refusal of an account the file does not list and the code-written title need them; `key_words` is the operation's name and the task's key. A refusal after the task was made cancels the request (see the review below), where the spec said it leaves it failed.
- **The card is said line by line, and the hash does not move.** An effect of the family stores `payload["card"]` (lines of `CARD_` keys) beside its English body; the messaging card says the lines in the project's language only when the card is well formed and its English lines are exactly the stored body (the card says what the terminal and the page show, and what the hash is the hash of); the fields are the person's or a service's words and are never translated. Stricter than the spec, which asked only for a valid card: the equality with the stored body, so that a payload that diverged from its body shows the body.
- **Limits.** L55 (a write only through the approval of the exact document, to a target `accounts` names), L57 (no permanent deletion) and L58 (a writer is named only by the effect modules and the one seam): for these two FND builds a static scan, with an empty allow-list, which is a MEASURE and not the limit; the limit is each writer's own `ALLOWED` and request function (see the review below). L56 and L59 to L62 are the packages' that build them; the numbers are the spec's and the supervisor orders them at the merge.
- **Not built here, on purpose.** No kind, no operation row, no group, no grant, no provider script: `EFFECT_APPROVAL_WORDS` and `effects.KINDS` gain a word only with its module (a test ties them), and the operations table gains a row only with its function.
- **After the independent review (#476).** (1) *The title is the code's, checked after the preparation:* `start` takes `title_key`, a phrase key of the family `TITLE_` with no field, so the title is fixed text, translated on a phone by `phrases.heading`; the opened decision is compared with it and with the rest of what the operation prepared (a well-formed `card` with no hidden character, whose English lines are its body, and the gate's effect word) and any mismatch fails closed: the request, its task and the decision are cancelled. (2) *Widening `accounts` is a gain that needs the hash (supervisor's decision, by recommendation, restrictive and reversible):* a new account, a changed address, a new calendar, folder or mail folder name, a changed id behind a name already there and `move_removes_inbox` turned on are lines of the widening list (`accounts:<provider>:<kind>:<name>=<id>`) and `accept-config` stops until the person types the hash, as for a policy gain; removals and narrowing need nothing. The record is its own cursor, `config:accounts`, one 10-character digest per item (a name and an id would not fit with the senders in one 4 KiB cursor); the digests of both records (accounts and the senders of POL-2) are KEYED: HMAC-SHA256 with a random project key (`config:digest-key`, made at the first acceptance, in the store outside the project), through one helper, `policy_widening.digest_of`. An UNKEYED short digest over text that a file's editor chooses can be attacked on purpose, not by accident: the search for a name or a sender id whose digest equals a held one takes about 2^40 / N tries at 40 bits (minutes on a laptop) and hides a gain; the keyed digest cannot be searched without the key, and the accidental collision (about 2^-40 per item, and 2^-48 per pair of senders) is left as it was. A record made before the key (`v` 2, plain sender ids) matches nothing once the key exists, so its senders are listed once at the next acceptance (restrictive); tests force a collision at a shortened length and show it hides a gain unkeyed and not keyed. (3) *The scans are measures, not the limits:* the L57 and L58 rows now say it. The code limit is each writer's own constant `ALLOWED` and request function with fake servers (the writers' packages); the scan reads syntax trees of the writers, the mailers of the person's account, the shared helpers, the authorize scripts and the readers, in any case, joined from pieces, as bytes, as a name or a library call, `trashed` in any form, and `runtime/` at any depth; an allowed DELETE is honoured only as a pair of the file's own `ALLOWED` constant with the regex the allow-list names, and a decoy elsewhere in the file is a finding. A script may delete its own record in the local secret store (`delete_password`), which is no data of an account. (4) *A refused start leaves nothing behind, and the request is cancelled, not failed:* a failed task keeps its request open for a retry that nothing makes (a start is always a new request), so a cap that held the task or a preparation that failed would have left a planned request and a ready or failed task per retry. `start` cancels the request, which cancels its task and any decision; the step's own sentence is raised, and the cause is in the sentence, not in a row. This differs from the spec's "leaves the request failed". (5) *Card fields refuse hidden characters:* a field with a control, format (a right-to-left override, a zero-width character), line or paragraph separator, surrogate or private-use character makes the card invalid (`phrases.card_valid`), except the line end and the tab in a field of `CARD_MULTILINE`; `start` refuses a card that is not valid, so the stored body is never shown with such a character. (6) *Ids:* a Google calendar id holds no `.`, `..` or `#` as a whole or part, and a OneDrive path holds no `#`, `%`, `?`, control or format character (so no percent-encoded form); the writers' rules in `providers/CONTRACT.md` say ids are percent-encoded with `safe=''` wherever they enter a URL. (7) *The operation belongs to the group of its word:* `calendar` is `calendar-write`, `mailbox` is `mail-organize`, `files` is `files-write`; a word whose group does not exist yet, or does not list the operation, starts nothing. (8) *The validator refuses a skill that declares a word only the runtime has:* `write`, `calendar`, `mailbox` and `files` are in the contract's table and in `SIDE_EFFECTS`, but not in `SKILL_EFFECTS`; a skill that declares one is an error of the rule `side-effects-vocabulary`, and `skill_import.py` stops on it. (9) *Brakes stop new work:* a messaging app cannot start an account write while the step brake (`set-mode --agent runtime`) is `stopped`; the person's own start from the terminal still runs. (10) *Not done:* the per-language rendering of the closed fields of a card (a status, a kind of file, a date) is not built; a kind that needs one adds a phrase key for it. `send` is out of the words of `start` (the outbox's `send-email` is not an account write); the package of the mail from the person's own account adds it with its group `mail-send`.
- **After the second review (#476).** (1) *The digests are keyed* (see the second item of the review above): one helper for the senders of POL-2 and the accounts, a project key made at the first acceptance, version 3 of the senders' record when keyed. (2) *The default calendar is in the record:* an event with no calendar named goes to the first calendar of the file, so the file's order decides it; the default is an item of the widening (`accounts:google:default-calendar=<name>`), and reordering the calendars is a gain that needs the hash. (3) *The line of a new account says what `move_removes_inbox` means* when it is on (the default): moving a message to a mail folder also takes it out of the Inbox. (4) The refusal of `accept-config` names the accounts; `start` cancels what it made also when it fails reading the card it opened; the L57 scan reads `"trashed": "true"`, concatenated or joined keys and joined method names. (5) *Forward note, not built:* a multi-line card field (`CARD_MULTILINE`) can put a second line that looks like another line of the card (a message body that holds "Account: ..."), and a combining character or a no-break space is not a hidden character; the W packages that carry a person's text on a card must frame it (its own start and end lines, as the card of a mail does) and `docs/backlog.md` says so.

## 2026-10-18: one token for the person's own Google account, and the restriction is the platform's (ACCT-G)

The maintainer decided on 2026-10-18 (SPEC-SECRETARY-WRITES, D-A and D-B) that the secretary manages everything the person has, so it must be able to read, edit and save in the person's own accounts, and that **security is done inside the platform, not by the APIs**: one OAuth client per provider asks the account for everything once, and the restriction comes from the platform's policies. This **reverses** three decisions of SPEC-SECRETARY-INTEGRATIONS (Q2, one registration per purpose; Q3, minimal scopes; section 4, write scopes in their own records) and the rule of `contracts/secrets.md` that a permission that writes lives in a separate secret from the one that reads, for the person's own Google and Microsoft accounts (the Microsoft half is package ACCT-M). The approval covered all fifteen recommendations of SPEC-SECRETARY-WRITES, among them the Google set below, one Google client for everything and the old records kept for one cycle.

- **The set.** `gmail.modify` ("does not allow immediate, permanent deletion of threads and messages, bypassing the trash"), `calendar.events`, `calendar.calendarlist.readonly` and `drive`, exactly those four, asked once by `providers/accounts/google_auth.py authorize`. The recommendation Q1 was approved: the pair `calendar.events` and `calendar.calendarlist.readonly` in place of the full `calendar` scope (whose text includes permanently deleting calendars), for the same reason `gmail.modify` is preferred to `https://mail.google.com/`. A token that lacks one of the four, or carries another, is refused (exit 3, naming the command); a refresh response that omits `scope` passes only when the record's stored scope is the four.
- **What was built.** `providers/_google.py` (the scope set and check, the one JSON record `GOOGLE_ACCOUNT_TOKEN` with its `slot`, the order in which a provider looks, the token endpoint, a scrub of every credential from printed text), `providers/accounts/google_auth.py` (`authorize`, `check`, `forget`, `--account main`; PKCE S256, loopback listener, the address read once from the Gmail profile) and `providers/accounts/README.md`. `gmail.py`, `calendar/google.py` and `documents/gdrive.py` read the account record first and their old record second; **their verbs are unchanged and send GET only**. `runtime/sources_mail.py`, `runtime/sources.py` and `runtime/sources_onedrive.py` hand each provider the account token of its service and its legacy names (Microsoft's too, so that ACCT-M touches no runtime file). Limit L59 (`contracts/runtime.md`) states it; the readers' part of limit L58 is tested in `providers/accounts/tests/test_account_google_readers.py`.
- **The token reaches everything; the platform decides what is reached.** Said plainly in `contracts/secrets.md`, the READMEs and `providers/accounts/README.md`: one Google token reads, changes, sends and trashes in Gmail, Calendar and Drive. What stops misuse is code: the token is read only by a provider process the runtime starts with that one secret, never in a run (L6, L59); the verbs each script exposes are allow-listed (the readers GET only; no script holds a permanent-delete call); every write of later packages is an effect whose exact content the person approves; and revoking the app's access in the Google Account stops every reader and writer of the account at once.
- **Legacy for one cycle.** Each Google reader prints on stderr, once per process, "using the legacy record <username>; run <authorize command> to move to the account token" when it fell back, and an account record that exists but is unreadable is an error, never a reason to fall back. The old `*_auth.py` commands are unchanged (their docstrings say "legacy"). The old records are never deleted by code. The fallback, the old commands and, with the full `drive` scope, the Picker are removed in the cycle after the integration branch reaches `main` (backlog ACCT-LEGACY). A second account later needs a registry row and `--account <slot>` (ACCT-SLOT); the hosted product's restricted-scope verification and security assessment are a later item (HOSTED-VERIFY).
- **Choices where the spec left one (by recommendation; the maintainer may revise).** (1) `check` calls nobody: it reads the record the way the providers do and compares its scope with the set (the readers' `--check` refresh the token). (2) The words ignored by the scope check are `openid`, `email`, `profile` and the two `userinfo` URLs Google returns for them: identity only. (3) The Picker keeps reading the legacy record explicitly (`gdrive.load_legacy_record`), because the account record has no Picker key. (4) The test overrides of the authorize command are `GOOGLE_ACCOUNT_API_BASE` and `GOOGLE_ACCOUNT_TOKEN_URL`, loopback only and set together, and then the OS secret store is never used. (5) `providers/accounts/` is not a class folder and has no implementation: `resolve.py` never lists it, and a test pins it. (6) The Drive 404 for an account token says "check the file id" and not "pick it again": under `drive` there is nothing to pick. (7) Nothing writes the Google record back (Google does not rotate a refresh token at use), so there is no cross-process lock; if a write-back is ever added, `authorize`, `forget` and the write-back take one `fcntl` file lock, as the review of the Microsoft side asked. (8) A record under `google-account` that is malformed, incomplete or names another slot is exit 3 naming `GOOGLE_ACCOUNT_TOKEN` and the store username, never a reason to fall back: the legacy record is used only when the account record is absent, and which record served is decided by the secret it was found under.
- **Cost of the safety.** One token that can do everything its scopes allow, accepted by the decision; a `drive` and a `gmail.modify` scope are Google restricted scopes, so the app stays "unverified" and the person clicks through a warning, limited to fewer than 100 users until the hosted product is verified; a legacy path that doubles the places a reader looks for one cycle. No skill, no agent and no fingerprinted file changed: no bump and no lab run; no call to Google was made (every test runs against a stand-in on loopback).

## 2026-10-18: the person's own calendar, created, changed and removed behind a card (W1)

Package W1 of SPEC-SECRETARY-WRITES, built on the foundation (FND) and the two account tokens (ACCT-G, ACCT-M). The maintainer approved the recommendations of the spec the same day; what that settled for this package: **Q1** the Google scope is `calendar.events` plus `calendar.calendarlist.readonly` (not the full `calendar`, whose text includes deleting and sharing whole calendars); **Q3** a change or a removal is allowed for an event the person organizes, with no other attendee and not recurring (a meeting or a series is refused in v1: changing a meeting tells other people, changing a series touches every occurrence); **Q4** no attendee on a create in v1 (an invitation is an e-mail to third parties; Graph "sends invitations to all attendees" and cannot be told not to); **Q11** the platform writes only to the calendars `runtime.json` `accounts` names; **Q12** removing an Outlook event is off (exit 2 of the writer, refused at the start, in the document and in `preflight`) until a live check shows a removed test event in Deleted Items. **Reversed by the maintainer on 2026-10-22** ("Apenas apague"): see the entry "removing an Outlook event is plain" below.

- **What was built.** The effect word `calendar` (the step kind `calendar-event`, `runtime/calendar_document.py` the pure half, `runtime/effect_calendar.py` the one place that writes, `runtime/ops_calendar.py` the three operations `event-create`, `event-change`, `event-cancel`), the group `calendar-write` and the grant `approve-effect-calendar` (in no default policy; the umbrella `approve-effects` implies the new grant), the read-only seam `calendar_read` of `runtime/ops_step_seams.py`, and the two writers of the class `writer:calendar` (`providers/writer/google-calendar.py`, `outlook-calendar.py`, with `providers/_writer_calendar.py` for what both share: the ledger, the spec file, the hash). Limits L55 to L59 are exercised by it; L56 (a change only to the event as it was read) is written into `contracts/runtime.md` with this package. No skill, no eval and no fingerprinted file changed: no bump and no lab run; every test runs against stand-ins on loopback and no Google or Microsoft host was called.
- **The request, and what the person approves.** The hash is the effect document's: the account, the writer, the calendar by name and id, the operation, the event id and the version the task read, the event as it was and as it will be, the zone, `send_updates: none`, the key, and the hash of the writer's dry run (the exact request it would send, no credential, no connection). `execute` refuses an approval of another hash, reads the event again and compares its version (`stale`), checks that the dry run still hashes to the document's, and only then sends `--confirmed`. A create is one POST; a change is one PATCH of the changed parts with `If-Match` (Google) or, on Outlook, which documents no If-Match, the writer reads the event and compares its changeKey right before the PATCH; a removal is one DELETE with `If-Match` (Google is expected to send the event to the calendar's trash for 30 days (the Calendar help page says so, the API page of the call does not: to verify live)). The words of the person (title, place, notes) reach the writer in a private file, never on a command line.
- **Choices where the spec left one (by recommendation; the maintainer may revise).** (1) An all-day event's `end` in the document is the last day it covers (the person's meaning); the request carries the day after, as both services want. (2) A start alone moves an event and keeps how long it lasts; an end alone keeps the start; changing between a timed event and an all-day one needs the start. (3) The local time the person gives is read in this machine's zone: the hour a clock change skips is refused, the hour it repeats is its first time; the card says the zone's name. (4) The key `cal-<32 hex>` derives from the whole document (the reading time included), so a task started over after a `stale` has a new key; a lost answer is found by the private property `openhora_key` (Google), an extended property (Outlook; to verify live) or the event's state (removal). (5) A change whose parts are already in the event (an attempt whose answer was lost) is not called stale: it goes on to the writer, which tells from its ledger (`replayed`) or refuses with `changed:`. (6) The dry run of a write verb is started without the account's token, in the preparation and in the approval alike; that stripped environment is not a barrier (a writer could still find a credential in the secret store), and what protects is the writer honouring `--dry-run` (no connection, no credential read), which its tests hold. (7) `describe`, which the state file's approval row holds, names the operation, the calendar's name and the account, never the title or the notes. (8) A reminder can be set and changed, not cleared (v1). (9) `event-change` and `event-cancel` are `job` rows of the table (they read the calendar in the preparation); `event-create` is not (its preparation opens no connection). (10) The page gained three routes (`/events`, `/events/change`, `/events/cancel`) because the rows list the page, and `interface/js/permissions.js` asks about `approve:effect:calendar`; no control is drawn. (11) The reference `created:<decision>` is resolved here from the decision's result; the reference `<source>#<n>` is resolved through the index of the snapshots (SNAP-IDX, `runtime/item_refs.py`): `CalendarReader.event_ref` calls the real `item_refs.resolve(ctx, ref, channel=, sender=, kinds=("calendar",), created=)` of SNAP-IDX: the account comes from the source row's implementation and the calendar id from the source row's origin (never the index), equal exactly to what `accounts` names; its refusals are phrases `ACCT_REF_*`; a `<source>#<n>` card says which snapshot it was read from. (13) Review of #479: a reference to an event FAILS CLOSED (it must name an account, and the calendar id it puts the event on must equal exactly the id `accounts` gives for the calendar asked for, else nothing is prepared); `event-change` and `event-cancel` take `calendar` and default to the calendar the reference names (a `created:` decision records its calendar); a reminder is not changed on an event with several reminders; a 503 on a write is unknown like any server error; an Outlook all-day event is read a second time in its own zone; ids never start with a hyphen.
